Study of a four dimensional Willmore-type energy
Peter Olamide Olanipekun

Abstract




A thesis submitted for the degree of

Doctor of Philosophy

at Monash University

2021

School of
Mathematics

Faculty of Science

Monash University

Melbourne, Australia

Copyright notice

© Peter Olamide Olanipekun (2021)

Abstract

In this thesis, a four dimensional conformally invariant energy is studied. This energy generalises the well known two-dimensional Willmore energy. Although not positive definite, it includes minimal hypersurfaces as critical points. We compute its first variation and by applying Noether’s theorem to the invariances, we derive some conservation laws which are satisfied by its critical points and with good analytical dispositions. In particular, we show that critical points are smooth. We also investigate other possible four dimensional generalisations of the Willmore energy, and give strong evidence that critical points of such energies do not include minimal hypersurfaces.

Decalaration

This thesis is an original work of my research and contains no material which has been accepted for the award of any other degree or diploma at any university or equivalent institution and that, to the best of my knowledge and belief, this thesis contains no material previously published or written by another person, except where due reference is made in the text of the thesis.

Print Name:  Peter Olamide Olanipekun

Date:       19 September, 2021

Chapter 1
Introduction

The goal of this Chapter is to present, in a simple (without technical details) fashion, the problems studied in this thesis. Relevant technical details will be sorted later in the appropriate Chapters. The main object of study in this thesis is an hypersurface critical for a certain four dimensional conformally invariant Willmore-type energy.

1.1
Brief historical context

The Willmore energy, was originally defined for two dimensional manifolds (or simply surfaces). The Willmore energy (also known as the Willmore functional) of a surface
\Sigma
immersed into the Euclidean space is given by the integral of the square of the mean curvature vector of
\Sigma
. That is,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}

(1.1)

where
d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
is the area element.
In broad terms, it measures the sphericity of a given surface.

The origin of the Willmore energy can be traced to Siméon-Denis Poisson and Sophie Germain who had engaged in a scientific contest to give a mathematical explanation of what is now known as the Chladni figures. The prize was won in 1816 by Sophie Germain.
She had studied the elasticity properties of membranes and thin plates and her work brought into limelight an entirely new area of applied mathematics, namely, elasticity theory. Her work shows that the bending energy of a thin plate, in its simplest form, can be written (using our notation) as (
1.1
) (see
[
27
]
). Problems involving elastic surfaces can be studied via the energy (
1.1
) (see
[
26
]
). Since then, the Willmore energy has been widely studied in differential geometry and applied to some areas of science such as General Relativity
[
38
,
41
]
(where the Willmore energy is the main contributor to the Hawking mass), Cell Biology
[
40
]
(where the Willmore energy plays a major role in the Helfrich model), String theory
[
59
]
, and Image Processing
[
44
]
. The Willmore energy also relates to the renormalised area functional for a minimal surfaces embedded in hyperbolic 3-manifold
[
2
]
.
Earlier works include those of G. Thomsen who established the Euler-Lagrange equation for the energy (
1.1
)
[
65
]
, W. Blaschke who observed minimal surfaces are minimizers of the energy (
1.1
)
[
11
]
, J. Weiner who generalised the work of Thomsen in higher codimension
[
66
]
and T. J. Willmore who explored the lower bounds for the Willmore energy of a closed orientable surface
[
68
]
. Actually, the Willmore energy was named after him.
Of great importance is the Willmore conjecture (
[
68
]
, see also
[
50
]
) which was proved in
[
52
]
by Fernando Codá Marques and André Neves.

Theorem 1.1.1
.

Let
\Sigma\subset S^{3}
be an embedded closed surface of genus
g\geq 1
. Then

\int_{\Sigma}(1+|\vec{H}|^{2})\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}\geq 2\pi^{2}

and the equality holds if and olnly if
\Sigma
is the Clifford torus up to conformal transformations of
S^{3}
.

Other notable contributions are those of B. Y. Chen
[
16
,
17
]
, J. H. White
[
67
]
, R. L. Bryant
[
13
]
, L. Simon
[
64
]
, U. Pinkall
[
58
]
, R. Kusner
[
47
]
, B. Palmer
[
56
,
57
]
, just to mention afew.

Recent efforts have been dedicated to the analysis of the critical points of the energy (
1.1
) under different conditions: in codimension one
[
19
,
48
]
, higher codimension
[
7
]
, in a fixed conformal class
[
12
,
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{sc}{}{}]}
]
and under other frameworks
[
5
,
6
,
8
,
10
,
61
]
.

1.2
The problem

Let
g
and
\vec{h}
denote the first and second fundamental form, respectively, on the immersed surface
\Sigma
. Let
\vec{h}_{0}:=\vec{h}-\vec{H}g
be the trace-free second fundamental form. Let
\chi(\Sigma)
be the Euler characteristic of the surface
\Sigma
. Owing to the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, the Willmore energy can be written as

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{h}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}+\pi\chi(\Sigma)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{h}_{0}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}+2\pi\chi(\Sigma)

(1.2)

so that the three energies appearing in (
1.2
) have the same critical points. Although the energy
\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{h}_{0}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
is conformally invariant, the other two energies in (
1.2
) are not.
The critical points of the energy (
1.1
) are found via variation of the energy. They satisfy the following Euler-Lagrange equation which is known in literature as the Willmore equation:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}-2|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}=\vec{0}

(1.3)

where
\Delta_{\perp}
is the negative covariant Laplacian for the Levi-Civita connection in the normal bundle. We will refer to the left hand side of (
1.3
) as the
Willmore invariant
. In codimension one, the above equation reduces to:

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}H+|h_{0}|^{2}H=0

(1.4)

where
\Delta_{g}
is the negative Laplace-Beltrami operator on
\Sigma
. Since the mean curvature depends on two derivatives of the immersion, the Willmore equation (
1.4
) (or more generally (
1.3
)) is a fourth order elliptic nonlinear partial differential equation. This leads us to the following interesting properties about the energy.

(a).

The Willmore energy is conformally invariant up to Gauss-Bonnet terms

(b).

The leading order operator in the Willmore equation is linear

(c).

Minimal surfaces are critical points of the Willmore energy

(d).

The Willmore energy is non-negative.

1.2.1
Finding a four dimensional Willmore-type energy

Generalising the Willmore energy to higher dimensions has been a question of interest for a long time. It has been often asked what properties or conditions must be satisfied by an energy that would qualify as a generalised higher dimensional version of the Willmore energy.
This essentially leads to the following problem in four dimensions:

Problem 1
.

Obtain a four dimensional Willmore-type energy preserving the properties (
a
)-(
d
) above.

By considering an energy involving a linear combination of the quantities

(\vec{h}_{0})_{ij}\cdot(\vec{h}_{0})^{jk}(\vec{h}_{0})_{kl}\cdot(\vec{h}_{0})^{li}\,,\quad(\vec{h}_{0})_{ij}\cdot(\vec{h}_{0})^{ij}(\vec{h}_{0})_{kl}\cdot(\vec{h}_{0})^{kl}\quad\mbox{and}\,((\vec{h}_{0})_{ij}\cdot(\vec{h}_{0})^{jk}(\vec{h}_{0})_{k}^{i})^{4/3}

one can easily construct a four dimensional conformally invariant energy. However, preliminary computations show that such energies do not preserve the properties (
b
) and (
c
) above (see for instance those considered in
[
60
]
and in Section 5 of
[
55
]
). Thus a different approach, which we shall now describe, is needed.

There is a relationship between Problem
1
and the singular Yamabe problem. Let
(M^{n},\bar{g})
be a smooth compact Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
with boundary and let
\Sigma
be an hypersurface, the singular Yamabe problem asks for a defining function
u
for the boundary
\Sigma:=\partial M
such that on the interior
\mathring{M}
, the scalar curvature
R_{g}
of the metric
g=u^{-2}\bar{g}
satisfies
R_{g}=-n(n-1)
.
This problem was first considered by Loewner and Nirenberg in
[
51
]
. Later,
Andersson, Chrúsciel and Friedrich
[
3
]
computed a conformal invariant which obstructs the smoothness of the function
u
. This obstruction to smooth boundary asymptotics for the Yamabe solution was identified by Gover et. al as a Willmore surface invariant in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see
[
30
,
31
,
33
]
). This identification led to the perception that higher dimensional generalisation of the Willmore invariant can be found via obstructions to smooth boundary asymptotics of the singular Yamabe problem on conformally compact manifolds of higher dimension. They further asked if there is a corresponding energy for such invariants, that is, is there a higher dimensional generalisation of the Willmore energy whose Euler-Lagrange equation corresponds to the identified obstruction? It turns out that the answer is positive (see
[
32
,
34
]
). Moreover, the following four dimensional generalisation of the Willmore energy was identified

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Sigma):=\int_{\Sigma}(|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}+7|\vec{H}|^{4})\,\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}

(1.5)

where notations have been slightly adjusted as follows:
\vec{h}_{ij}:=\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}
,
\vec{H}:=\frac{1}{4}g^{ij}\vec{h}_{ij}=\vec{h}^{j}_{j}
,
|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|:=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H}
, where
\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}
is the projection of the Levi-Civita connection, acting on
\vec{H}
, onto the normal bundle,
|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}:=(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})
and
|\vec{H}|^{4}:=(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{H})^{2}
. This energy was first discussed in codimension one
1
1

1

In codimension one, the energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
reduces to
\int_{\Sigma}(|\nabla H|-|H|^{2}|h|^{2}+7|H|^{4})\,\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
.
by Guven in
[
37
]
and rediscovered by Robin-Graham and Reichert in
[
35
]
, and separately by Zhang in
[
70
]
. The energy (
1.5
) satisfies the conditions (
a
)-(
c
) specified above; infact, it satisfies (
a
) without Gauss-Bonnet. However, as noted in
[
35
]
, it is not bounded below, thus violating condition (
d
). It is possible to construct a non-negative energy by adding, to the energy (
1.5
), a multiple of the fourth power of the norm of the trace-free second fundamental form. In such situation, one considers the energy:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{1}(\Sigma):=\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)+\mu\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}

(1.6)

for some
\mu\geq\frac{1}{12}
. But one discovers that the energy
\mathcal{E}^{1}(\Sigma)
violates the very crucial condition (
c
) (see Section
2.3
). Thus, in this thesis, we will focus on studying the critical points of the energy (
1.5
).

1.2.2
Conservation laws

The two dimensional Willmore energy is finite as soon as the Willmore immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
belongs to the Sobolev space
W^{1,\infty}\cap W^{2,2}
. This implies that
\vec{h}\in L^{2}
.
This fact, when incorporated into the Willmore equation, gives no analytical meaning as the cubic curvature terms
(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h})\vec{h}-2|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}
(or more importantly
\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}
as per (
1.3
)) would not even belong to
L^{1}
. However, this issue can be resolved with the help of conservation laws.
In the seminal article
[
61
]
, the author found such conservation laws. It was shown that the fourth-order Willmore equation, associated with the two-dimensional Willmore energy, can be expressed in divergence forms with several analytical implications. These divergence forms were elegantly obtained, in
[
7
]
, by applying Noether’s theorem (a general result applicable in any dimension) to the invariances of the Willmore energy and several other pre-existing results were rediscovered. Indeed, there exist some 1-forms
\vec{V}_{\textnormal{tra}}
,
{V}_{\textnormal{dil}}
and
\vec{V}_{\textnormal{rot}}
such that

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{W}}=d^{\star}\vec{V}_{\textnormal{tra}}=\vec{0}\,\,,\quad d^{\star}{V}_{\textnormal{dil}}={0}\,\,,\quad d^{\star}\vec{V}_{\textnormal{rot}}=\vec{0}

(1.7)

where
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
is the Willmore invariant. The quantity
\vec{V}_{\textnormal{tra}}
is geometric since it depends on
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
. The quantities
{V}_{\textnormal{dil}}
and
\vec{V}_{\textnormal{rot}}
are also geometric since they depend on
\vec{V}_{\textnormal{tra}}
.
Applying Poincaré lemma to (
1.7
), one finds the primitives
\vec{L}
,
S
and
\vec{R}
satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}d^{\star}\vec{L}=\vec{V}_{\textnormal{tra}}\,\,,\quad d^{\star}{S}={V}_{\textnormal{dil}}\,\,,&d^{\star}\vec{R}=\vec{V}_{\textnormal{rot}}\\
d\vec{L}=\vec{0}\,\,,\quad\quad\quad d{S}={0}\,\,,&d\vec{R}=\vec{0}\\
\end{array}\right.

where the Hodge star
\star
, the exterior differential
d
and codifferential
d^{\star}
are understood in terms of the induced metric
g
.
Further computations
[
7
]
yield the following system
2
2

2

The geometric product
\bullet
is defined in Section
2.1
of Chapter
2
.
of second-order partial differential equations:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|g|^{1/2}\Delta_{g}(\star S)&=\star\big(d(\star\vec{n})\cdot d(\star\vec{R})\big)\\
|g|^{1/2}\Delta_{g}(\star\vec{R})&=\star\big(d(\star\vec{n})\bullet d(\star\vec{R})\big)+\star\big(d(\star\vec{n})\,\,d(\star{S})\big)\\
|g|^{1/2}\Delta_{g}\vec{\Phi}&=\star\big(d(\star\vec{R})\bullet d\vec{\Phi}\big)+\star\big(d(\star{S})\,\,d\vec{\Phi}\big)\\
\end{array}\right.

where
\vec{n}
denotes the Gauss map on
\Sigma
. The system (
1.2.2
) possesses a Jacobian structure suitable for analysis
[
6
,
7
,
9
,
61
]
. These formulations and results lead us to ask if analogous results can be obtained in four dimensions. In particular, we are interested in the following problem.

Problem 2
.

What conservation laws are satisfied by the critical points of the energy (
1.5
)?

Understanding Willmore-type equations in higher dimensions, from analytical perspective, can be generally a difficult problem.
For instance, the Willmore-type equation associated with (
1.5
) is a sixth-order nonlinear partial differential equation, the study of which is rare in literature.

In Chapter
2
, we will use Noether’s theorem and the fact that the energy (
1.5
) is invariant under coordinate transformation to derive some conservation laws satisfied by the critical points of (
1.5
). These laws are particularly helpful in analysing the complicated sixth-order Willmore-type equation.

1.2.3
Regularity of critical points

The question of regularity of the two dimensional Willmore surface is already settled in literature. For instance, it was proved in
[
9
]
and
[
61
]
that Willmore immersions associated with the two dimensional Willmore energy are smooth. The proof techniques heavily rely on the structure of the system (
1.2.2
). The regularity assumptions on
\vec{\Phi}
implies that
\vec{n}
,
S
and
\vec{R}
belong to the Sobolev space
W^{1,2}.
Thus it follows that
\Delta_{g}(\star S)
,
\Delta_{g}(\star\vec{R})
and
\Delta_{g}\vec{\Phi}
all belong to
L^{1}
; this information is critical for regularity purpose. However, the Jacobian structure of system (
1.2.2
) encodes an extra regularity. By using Wente estimates (see Chapter 3 of
[
39
]
,
[
9
]
), one improves the regularity of
(\star S)
and
(\star\vec{R})
by showing that they both belong to
W^{1,p}
for some
p>2
.
The third equation in (
1.2.2
) links the primitives
(\star S)
and
(\star\vec{R})
back to the Willmore immersion
\vec{\Phi}
. In fact, it follows that
\vec{\Phi}
belongs to
W^{2,p}
. By applying a bootstrapping argument, one concludes that
\vec{\Phi}
is smooth.
This leads us to ask if such procedure can be applied in four dimensions. Essentially, we have:

Problem 3
.

Show that the critical points of the energy (
1.5
) are smooth.

It is interesting to note that the proof techniques employed in resolving Problem
3
is remarkably different from the regularity proof for the two dimensional Willmore surfaces. Unlike the two dimensional case, the corresponding expressions for
\vec{L}
,
S
and
\vec{R}
are much more intricate. Also, the system of equations does not seem to possess Jacobian structure, hence we do not use Wente estimates in our proof. However with the help of a Bourgain-Brezis type result and classical results of Miranda
[
54
]
and Di Fazio
[
22
]
, we are able to obtain preliminary results needed to begin the regularity proof.

In summary, we have made a progress towards the analytical study of the Willmore-type energy (
1.5
). This energy is new in literature and has not been analytically explored.

1.3
Outline of chapters

In Chapter
1
, we introduced the problems considered in this thesis. The main object of interest is the four dimensional Willmore-type energy.

The content of Chapter
2
is computational, and heavily employs conventions from tensors and differential forms. We use variational techniques to compute the Euler-Lagrange equation (which we shall often refer to as the
Willmore-Euler-Lagrange equation
or simply the
Willmore-type equation
3
3

3

More specifically, we shall often refer to analogous Willmore concepts in four dimensions as
Willmore-type
.
) for the energy (
1.5
); this is basically Theorem
2.2.2
. Theorem
2.4.2
gives conservation laws for the energy (
1.5
) in higher codimension (or for Willmore-type submanifolds). Conformal invariance of the energy is established in Theorem
2.5.1
. In Proposition
2.6.3
, we restrict our computations to codimension one and establish conservation laws satisfied by the Willmore-type hypersurfaces. In view of Problem
1
, in Section
2.3
we also present other possible four dimensional generalisations of the energy (
1.5
) and argue that the only energy whose critical points include minimal hypersurfaces is the energy (
1.5
); the other energies violate the condition (
c
). This explains the reason for the choice of the energy (
1.5
) in this study.

In Chapter
3
, we take the first step towards the analysis of the energy (
1.5
). We prove (Theorem
3.0.1
) that the Willmore-type hypersurface is indeed smooth. This is done in a series of steps organised into Sections
3.2
-
3.5
. In Chapter
4
, we discuss other analysis aspects of the energy (
1.5
), all of which are open problems to the best of our knowledge. Thus, Chapter
4
presents suggestions for further research. We include an Appendix where some basic notions in Riemannian geometry relevant to our study are recalled and some useful computations are elaborated.

Notational conventions.
Unless otherwise specified, we will adopt the following notations and conventions in this work.

1.

The Greek letter
\vec{\Phi}
will be reserved for immersions of
\Sigma
. In two dimensions,
\Sigma
will denote a closed surface but in four dimensions it will denote a closed hypersurface or submanifold, depending on the specified codimension.

2.

The induced metric or the first fundamental form will be denoted by
g
with volume element
d\textnormal{vol}_{g}:=|g|^{1/2}dx
. The component of the first and second fundamental forms are
g_{ij}
and
\vec{h}_{ij}
respectively. The mean curvature is denoted
\vec{H}
and is defined
\vec{H}:=\frac{1}{n}g^{ij}\vec{h}_{ij}
where
n
is the dimension of
\Sigma
.

3.

We will use the Greek letter
\delta
to denote the variation of a quantity. The Greek letter
\delta
with indices will be reserved for the component of the flat metric. For example,
\delta g_{ij}
is the variation of the
(i,j)
component of the metric
g
; while
\delta^{i}_{j}
is the
(i,j)
component of the flat metric.

4.

We adopt Einstein’s summation convention for our computations.

5.

We denote by
\pi_{\vec{n}}(\vec{V})
and
\pi_{T}(\vec{V})
the normal and tangential projections respectively of some vector
\vec{V}
. We have
\pi_{\vec{n}}+\pi_{T}=\textnormal{id}
.

6.

Also, we use the metric tensor
g_{ij}
(or the inverse metric tensor
g^{ij}
) to lower (or raise) indices on tensors. For example,
\mathchoice{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{j}{l}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{j}{l}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{j}{l}}}}{T^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{j}{l}}}}=g_{ij}g_{kl}T^{ik}
and
\mathchoice{T^{{{j}{l}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{T^{{{j}{l}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{T^{{{j}{l}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{T^{{{j}{l}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=g^{ij}g^{kl}T_{ik}
.

7.

The operators
\Delta
,
\Delta_{g}
and
\Delta_{0}
will denote the Hodge Laplacian, the Laplace Beltrami and the flat Laplacian respectively. The operators
\Delta
and
\Delta_{g}
coincide when they act on “scalar” functions.

Chapter 2
Variational Derivations and Conservation Laws

2.1
Introduction

In this chapter, we derive the Willmore-Euler-Lagrange equation for the Robin-Graham-Reichert energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
of (
1.5
). This system of sixth-order, elliptic, nonlinear equations was first obtained in
[
35
]
and
[
70
]
in arbitrary codimension
1
1

1

There is a mistake in
[
35
]
, in higher codimension, which we will correct in due time.
. In order to study this system of equations
2
2

2

We will study the Willmore-Euler-Lagrange equation in codimension one. However, our method works in higher codimension at the cost of really cumbersome notations.
, we follow Bernard’s formalism
[
7
]
to establish conservation laws satisfied by the critical points of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
under translation, dilation and rotation. Indeed, it is clear that the energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
is invariant under translation, dilation and rotation. From this, we deduce that the Willmore-type operator (the left hand side of (
2.1
)) is actually the divergence of a specific vector field and we write

\mathcal{\vec{W}}=\nabla_{j}\vec{V}^{j}

where the quantities
\mathcal{\vec{W}}
and
\vec{V}
(the stress-energy tensor for
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
) are made precise in Sections
2.2
and
2.4
.

Special conformal transformation can be obtained by performing an inversion, a translation and an inversion again (in that order). Hence, we prove, in Section
2.5
, that
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
is conformally invariant by showing that
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
is invariant under special conformal transformation, following the method of Guven in
[
37
]
.
The need for the stress energy tensor (obtained in Section
2.4
), in the proof of conformal invariance, explains why the content of Section
2.5
comes after Section
2.4
.
Finally in Section
2.6
, we obtain further conservation laws by proving the existence of some 2-forms needed to write some useful equations.

The major achievement of this Chapter is the reduction of the complicated sixth-order elliptic nonlinear Willmore-Euler-Lagrange equation for
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
to a manageable system of second order partial differential equations.
Although this Chapter is largely computational, it prepares the ground for the local analysis of critical points of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
.

We now briefly explain some of the notations and conventions adopted in this Chapter. The
small dot operator

\cdot
is used to denote the usual dot product between vectors. We also use the small dot to denote the natural extension of the dot product in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
to multivectors (see
[
25
]
). We define the bilinear map

{{{{\llcorner}}}}:\Lambda^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\times\Lambda^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\rightarrow\Lambda^{p-q}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
, known as the
interior multiplication
of multivectors, by

\langle\vec{A}\,\,{{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{B},\,\vec{C}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}=\langle\vec{A},\vec{B}\wedge\vec{C}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}

where
\vec{A}\in\Lambda^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
\vec{B}\in\Lambda^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
\vec{C}\in\Lambda^{p-q}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
with
p\geq q
. If
p=1=q
then we have the usual dot product. Here, we have used the notation
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
to emphasise the inner product of multivectors in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We will reserve the notation
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
for the inner product of forms on patches of the manifold, this will be defined and used in Chapter
3
.

The
bullet dot operator

\bullet:\Lambda^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\times\Lambda^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\rightarrow\Lambda^{p+q-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
is well known in geometric algebra as the first-order contraction operator. For a
k
-vector
\vec{A}
and a
1
-vector
\vec{B}
, we define

\vec{A}\bullet\vec{B}=\vec{A}{{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{B}.

For a
p
-vector
\vec{B}
and a
q
-vector
\vec{C}
, we have

\vec{A}\bullet(\vec{B}\wedge\vec{C})=(\vec{A}\bullet\vec{B})\wedge\vec{C}+(-1)^{pq}(\vec{A}\bullet\vec{C})\wedge\vec{B}.

Recall that we are adopting Einstein’s summation convention for our computations. Also, we will use the metric tensor (or the metric inverse) to lower (or raise) indices on tensors.

2.2
Willmore-Euler-Lagrange type equation

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be an immersion of a 4-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
. Let
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
be the Willmore-type energy

\mathcal{E}(\Sigma):=\int_{\Sigma}\left(|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}+7|\vec{H}|^{4}\right)\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

The manifold
\Sigma
is Willmore-type for
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
if it is a critical point of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma).

An important characterisation of Willmore-type immersions can be deduced from the Willmore-Euler-Langrange equation associated with
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
which we now state as the main result of this section.

Theorem 2.2.2
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be an immersion of a 4-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
.

\vec{\Phi}
is Willmore-type for
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
if and only if
\vec{\Phi}
satisfies the following Willmore-Euler-Lagrange equation

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{W}}=\vec{0}

(2.1)

where

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}_{\perp}\vec{H}-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{h}_{ik}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H})\vec{h}^{ik}-4|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}+2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}((\vec{h}^{j}_{i}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})\vec{H})

\displaystyle\quad-2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{i})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H})+2(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H})\vec{h}^{ij}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij})-2\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{H})-28|\vec{H}|^{4}\vec{H}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}_{pq})\vec{h}^{pq}-4(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{k})\vec{h}^{jk}+4|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}\vec{H}

\displaystyle\quad+7\Delta_{\perp}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})+7|\vec{H}|^{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}

where
\Delta_{\perp}
is the negative covariant Laplacian for the Levi-Civita connection in the normal bundle.

Remark 2.2.3
.

As already pointed out by Zhang
[
70
]
and Robin-Graham-Reichert
[
35
]
, examples of critical points of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
include minimal submanifolds, totally geodesic submanifolds
\Sigma
of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, round spheres
\mathbb{S}^{4}
of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and products of spheres of radius
r
such as:
\mathbb{S}^{2}(r_{1})\times\mathbb{S}^{2}(r_{2})
,
\mathbb{S}^{1}(r_{1})\times\mathbb{S}^{3}(r_{2})
,
\mathbb{S}^{1}(r_{1})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}(r_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{2}(r_{3})
and
\mathbb{S}^{1}(r_{1})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}(r_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}(r_{3})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}(r_{4})
where
\sum r_{i}^{2}=1.

Indeed, these are all solutions of the sixth order non-linear elliptic partial differential equation (
2.1
).

The proof of Theorem
2.2.2
relies on the following crucial lemma.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a smooth immersion of a 4-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
and
\vec{\Phi}_{s}
a variation of the form
\vec{\Phi}_{s}=\vec{\Phi}+s(A^{j}\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}+\vec{B})
, where
\vec{B}
is a normal vector. Denote by
g
and
\vec{h}
the first and second fundamental forms respectively. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\delta g_{ij}=\nabla_{i}A_{j}+\nabla_{j}A_{i}-2\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij}

(2.2)

\displaystyle\delta g^{ij}=-\nabla^{i}A^{j}-\nabla^{j}A^{i}+2\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij}

(2.3)

\displaystyle\delta|g|^{1/2}=|g|^{1/2}(\nabla_{j}A^{j}-4\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H})

(2.4)

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{h}_{ij}=A^{k}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{k}\vec{h}_{ij}+\nabla_{i}A^{k}\vec{h}_{ik}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}

(2.5)

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}=\frac{1}{4}(4A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}+\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B})

(2.6)

Proof.

Consider a variation of the type

\delta\vec{\Phi}=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\vec{\Phi}_{t}-\vec{\Phi}}{t}:=A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+\vec{B}

where
\vec{B}
is a normal vector.

The metric
g
varies as follows.

\displaystyle\delta g_{ij}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}\delta\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}+\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{j}\delta\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}(A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+\vec{B})\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}+\nabla_{j}(A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+\vec{B})\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}A_{j}+\nabla_{j}A_{i}+\nabla_{i}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}+\nabla_{j}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}A_{j}+\nabla_{j}A_{i}-2\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij}.

The inverse metric
g^{-1}
varies as follows. Observe that

\displaystyle\delta g^{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta(g^{ik}g^{jl}g_{kl})

\displaystyle=\delta g^{ik}\delta^{j}_{k}+\delta^{i}_{l}\delta g^{jl}+g^{ik}g^{jl}\delta g_{kl}

\displaystyle=2\delta g^{ij}+g^{ik}g^{jl}\delta g_{kl}

so that

\displaystyle\delta g^{ij}

\displaystyle=-g^{ik}g^{jl}\delta g_{kl}

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{i}A^{j}-\nabla^{j}A^{i}+2\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij}.

The variation of the square root of the determinant of the metric is found via the well-known Jacobi formula.

\displaystyle\delta|g|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|g|^{-1/2}\delta|g|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|g|^{-1/2}|g|\textnormal{Tr}(g^{-1}\delta g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|g|^{1/2}g^{ij}(\nabla_{i}A_{j}+\nabla_{j}A_{i}-2\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})

\displaystyle=|g|^{1/2}(\nabla_{j}A^{j}-4\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H}).

Next, we obtain the normal projection of the variation of the second fundamental form.
Note that

\delta\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}=\nabla_{j}\delta\vec{\Phi}=\nabla_{j}(A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+\vec{B})=A^{s}\vec{h}_{sj}+\nabla_{j}A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+\nabla_{j}\vec{B}.

Using the well-known Codazzi-Mainardi equations

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{h}_{jk}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{h}_{ik}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{k}\vec{h}_{ij}

(2.7)

we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{h}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\delta\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\left(\nabla_{j}A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+A^{s}\vec{h}_{js}+\nabla_{j}\vec{B}\right)

\displaystyle=A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{h}_{sj}+\nabla_{i}A^{s}\vec{h}_{sj}+\nabla_{j}A^{s}\vec{h}_{is}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{s}\vec{h}_{ij}+\nabla_{i}A^{s}\vec{h}_{sj}+\nabla_{j}A^{s}\vec{h}_{is}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}.

Finally, the normal projection of the variation of the mean curvature vector is obtained via the Codazzi-Mainardi equation (
2.7
). Note that

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\delta\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\vec{h}_{sj}\nabla^{j}A^{s}+A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{h}_{sj}+\vec{h}^{j}_{s}\nabla_{j}A^{s}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\Delta_{g}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=2\vec{h}_{sj}\nabla^{j}A^{s}+4A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\Delta_{g}\vec{B}.

(2.8)

By using (
2.3
) and (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\nabla^{j}\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}=\frac{1}{4}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta(g^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(\vec{h}_{ij}\delta g^{ij}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\delta\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\vec{h}_{ij}(-\nabla^{i}A^{j}-\nabla^{j}A^{i}+2\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}(2\vec{h}_{sj}\nabla^{j}A^{s}+4A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{s}\vec{\vec{H}}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\Delta_{g}\vec{B})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(4A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+2(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\Delta_{g}\vec{B})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(4A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}+\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}).

∎

Proof of Theorem
2.2.2
.

Our approach is to compute the variation of each term of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
separately on a small patch
\Sigma_{0}
of the manifold
\Sigma
. First, we consider the variation of
|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}
.
Given a tensor field
\vec{T}_{j}
, the variation of
|\vec{T}|^{2}
is found from the variation of
\vec{T}_{j}
as follows:

\displaystyle\delta|\vec{T}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\delta(g^{ij}\vec{T}_{i}\cdot\vec{T}_{j})=(\vec{T}_{i}\cdot\vec{T}_{j})\delta g^{ij}+2\vec{T}^{j}\cdot\delta\vec{T}_{j}

\displaystyle=2\vec{T}_{i}\cdot\vec{T}_{j}(-\nabla^{i}A^{j}+\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})+2\vec{T}^{j}\cdot\delta\vec{T}_{j}

where we have used (
2.3
). Using (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle|g|^{-1/2}\delta(|\vec{T}|^{2}|g|^{1/2})

\displaystyle=2\vec{T}_{i}\cdot\vec{T}_{j}(-\nabla^{i}A^{j}+\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})+2\vec{T}^{j}\cdot\delta\vec{T}_{j}+|\vec{T}|^{2}(\nabla_{j}A^{j}-4\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H})

(2.9)

We will now vary
|\vec{T}|^{2}|g|^{1/2}
for the choice
\vec{T}_{j}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}
. We will need (
2.6
). First note that

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\delta\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\delta\nabla_{j}\vec{H}-\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\delta\pi_{T}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\delta\vec{H}-\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\delta\pi_{T}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{T}\delta\vec{H}-\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\delta\pi_{T}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\left[\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}[(\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\delta\vec{H})\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}]-\delta[(\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{H})\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}]\right]

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}+\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\left[-\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}[(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\delta\vec{\Phi})\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}]+\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta[(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{j})\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}]\right]

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}-\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\left[(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\delta\vec{\Phi})\vec{h}^{i}_{j}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{j}^{i})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\delta\vec{\Phi}\right]

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}-\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\left[[\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}(A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+\vec{B})]\vec{h}^{i}_{j}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{j})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}(A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+\vec{B})\right]

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}-\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\left[(A^{s}\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{is}+\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})\vec{h}^{i}_{j}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{j})(A^{s}\vec{h}_{is}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{B})\right]

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}-A^{i}\left[(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{is})(\vec{h}^{s}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{s}_{j})(\vec{h}_{is}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})(\vec{h}^{i}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{j})(\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{B}).

(2.10)

By using Simon’s identity

\Delta\vec{h}_{ij}=\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}+\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ik}g^{kl}\vec{h}_{lj}-|\vec{h}|^{2}\vec{h}_{ij}

we see that

\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}=\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}.

Hence, we find that

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}-\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{is})\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\pi_{T}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{is})(\vec{h}^{s}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{i}|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{sj})\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi})+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{is})(\vec{h}^{s}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{i}|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{sj})(\vec{h}^{s}_{i}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{is})(\vec{h}^{s}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{i}|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{s}_{j})(\vec{h}_{is}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{is})(\vec{h}^{s}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})

(2.11)

Using (
2.11
) in (
2.10
) yields

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\delta\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\left[\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{H}-A^{i}\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\right]+\frac{A^{i}}{2}\nabla_{i}|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})(\vec{h}^{i}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{j})(\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{B}).

(2.12)

Calling upon (
2.6
) now gives

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\delta\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\left[\frac{1}{4}\nabla_{j}(4A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik}+\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B})-A^{i}\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})(\vec{h}^{i}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{j})(\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{B})+\frac{A^{i}}{2}\nabla_{i}|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H})\nabla_{j}A^{i}+\frac{1}{4}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}((\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}-(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})(\vec{h}^{i}_{j}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{j})(\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{B})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{A^{i}}{2}\nabla_{i}|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\vec{T}_{i}\cdot\vec{T}_{j})\nabla^{j}A^{i}+\frac{A^{i}}{2}\nabla_{i}|\vec{T}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\vec{T}^{j}\cdot\nabla_{j}((\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})+\frac{1}{4}\vec{T}^{j}\cdot\nabla_{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}

\displaystyle\quad-(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})(\vec{h}^{i}_{j}\cdot\vec{T}^{j})+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{j})(\vec{T}^{j}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B}).

(2.13)

Substituting (
2.13
) into (
2.9
) gives

\displaystyle|g|^{-1/2}\delta(|\vec{T}|^{2}|g|^{1/2})

\displaystyle=2(\vec{T}_{i}\cdot\vec{T}_{j})(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})+\frac{1}{2}\vec{T}^{j}\cdot\nabla_{j}((\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})+\frac{1}{2}\vec{T}^{j}\cdot\nabla_{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}

\displaystyle\quad-2(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{B})(\vec{h}^{j}_{i}\cdot\vec{T}^{i})+2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{i})(\vec{T}^{i}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{B})-4|\vec{T}|^{2}\vec{H}\cdot\vec{B}+\nabla^{j}(|\vec{T}|^{2}A_{j})

\displaystyle=\vec{B}\cdot\left[2(\vec{T}_{i}\cdot\vec{T}_{j})\vec{h}^{ij}-4|\vec{T}|^{2}\vec{H}-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{h}_{ik}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{T}^{j})\vec{h}^{ik}+2\nabla_{j}((\vec{T}^{i}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{i})\vec{H})\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\left.-2\nabla_{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{i})\vec{T}^{i})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{T}^{i}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla^{j}\left[|\vec{T}|^{2}A_{j}+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})(\vec{T}_{j}\cdot\vec{h}_{ik})-2(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H})(\vec{T}^{i}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\left.+2(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{T}^{i})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})+\frac{1}{2}\vec{T}_{j}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{T}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{T}^{i}\right].

(2.14)

Equation (
2.14
) can be simplified by noting that

\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{T}^{i}=\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle|g|^{-1/2}\delta(|\vec{T}|^{2}|g|^{1/2})=\vec{B}\cdot\mathcal{\vec{W}}_{1}+\nabla^{j}(V_{1})_{j}

(2.15)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{\vec{W}}_{1}

\displaystyle=2(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H})\vec{h}^{ij}-4|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{h}_{ik}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H})\vec{h}^{ik}

\displaystyle+2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}((\vec{h}^{j}_{i}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})\vec{H})-2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{i})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}_{\perp}\vec{H}

and

\displaystyle(V_{1})_{j}=|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}A_{j}+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})(\vec{h}_{ik}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{H})-2(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})

\displaystyle+2(\vec{B}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})+\frac{1}{2}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}.

Next is to compute the variation of
|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}
. We consider an energy involving
|T|^{2}
for a symmetric 2-tensor
T_{ij}
. We have

\displaystyle\delta|T|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta(g^{ik}g^{jl}T_{kl}T_{ij})=2\left(T^{ij}\delta T_{ij}+T_{ij}T_{k}^{i}\delta g^{jk}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2T^{ij}\delta T_{ij}+2T_{ij}T^{i}_{k}\left(-\nabla^{j}A^{k}-\nabla^{k}A^{j}+2\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk}\right).

By using (
2.5
) and (
2.6
), the variation of
T_{ij}
for the choice
T_{ij}:=\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij}
is

\displaystyle\delta{T}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})

(2.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}A^{s}\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{sj}+\nabla_{j}A^{s}\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{is}+A^{s}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{s}\vec{h}_{ij}+A^{s}\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\nabla_{s}\vec{H}

\displaystyle+\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}+\frac{1}{4}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{pq})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}_{pq})+\frac{1}{4}\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{sj}\nabla_{i}A^{s}+T_{is}\nabla_{j}A^{s}+A^{s}\nabla_{s}T_{ij}

\displaystyle+\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}+\frac{1}{4}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{pq})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}_{pq})+\frac{1}{4}\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}.

Using (
2.4
) and (
2.16
), we find

\displaystyle|g|^{-1/2}\delta\left(|T|^{2}|g|^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(T^{ij}\delta T_{ij}+2T_{ij}T^{i}_{k}\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk}-T_{ij}T^{i}_{k}\nabla^{j}A^{k}-T_{ij}T^{i}_{k}\nabla^{k}A^{j}\right)

\displaystyle+|T|^{2}\nabla_{j}A^{j}-4|T|^{2}\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{s}\left(A^{s}|T|^{2}\right)+2T^{ij}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}+\frac{1}{2}T^{ij}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{pq})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}_{pq})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}T^{ij}\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}+4T_{ij}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{k}^{i})(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})-4|T|^{2}\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{B}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{2}+\nabla_{k}(V_{2})^{k}

where

\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{2}:=2\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}(T^{ik}\vec{H})+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}(T^{ij}\vec{h}_{ij})+\frac{1}{2}T^{ij}(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}_{pq})\vec{h}^{pq}+4T_{ij}T^{i}_{k}\vec{h}^{jk}-4|T|^{2}\vec{H}

and

(V_{2})^{k}:=2T^{ik}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B}-2\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{i}(T^{ik}\vec{H})+\frac{1}{2}T^{ij}\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\nabla^{k}\vec{B}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla^{k}(T^{ij}\vec{h}_{ij})+A^{k}|T|^{2}.

Finally, we consider the variation of
|\vec{H}|^{4}
. Using (
2.6
) we have

\displaystyle\delta|\vec{H}|^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\cdot\delta\vec{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\cdot\left(4A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}+\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}\right).

Hence, using (
2.4
), we find

\displaystyle|g|^{-1/2}\delta\left(|\vec{H}|^{4}|g|^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4|\vec{H}|^{2}A^{s}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+|\vec{H}|^{2}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})

\displaystyle\quad+|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}+|\vec{H}|^{4}\nabla_{j}A^{j}-4|\vec{H}|^{4}\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{B}\cdot\mathcal{\vec{W}}_{3}+\nabla_{j}(V_{3})^{j}.

where

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{3}=\Delta_{\perp}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})+|\vec{H}|^{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}-4|\vec{H}|^{4}\vec{H}

and

\displaystyle(V_{3})^{j}=|\vec{H}|^{4}A^{j}+|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{B}-\vec{B}\cdot\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}).

By setting

\vec{\mathcal{W}}:=\vec{\mathcal{W}_{1}}-\vec{\mathcal{W}_{2}}+7\vec{\mathcal{W}_{3}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{V}^{j}:=({V}_{1})^{j}-({V}_{2})^{j}+7({V}_{3})^{j}

we see that

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle=2(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{H})\vec{h}^{ij}-4|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{h}_{ik}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H})\vec{h}^{ik}+2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}((\vec{h}^{j}_{i}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})\vec{H})

\displaystyle\quad-2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{i})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}_{\perp}\vec{H}-2\nabla_{i}\nabla_{k}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{H})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}_{pq})\vec{h}^{pq}-4(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{i}_{k})\vec{h}^{jk}+4|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}\vec{H}

\displaystyle\quad+7\Delta_{\perp}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})+7|\vec{H}|^{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}-28|\vec{H}|^{4}\vec{H}

and

\displaystyle V^{j}

\displaystyle=\left(|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}+7|\vec{H}|^{4}\right)A^{j}+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})(\vec{h}_{ik}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})-2(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H})(\vec{h}^{j}_{i}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})

\displaystyle\quad+2(\vec{B}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{B}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla^{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}

\displaystyle\quad-2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})+2\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{i}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H})-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{B}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}\cdot\nabla^{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})+7|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{B}-7\vec{B}\cdot\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}).

Moreover, it follows that

\displaystyle|g|^{-1/2}\delta\left((|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}+7|\vec{H}|^{4})|g|^{1/2}\right)=\vec{B}\cdot\mathcal{\vec{W}}+\nabla_{j}V^{j}

(2.17)

or more specifically

\displaystyle\delta\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}+7|\vec{H}|^{4})\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}=\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(\vec{B}\cdot\mathcal{\vec{W}}+\nabla_{j}V^{j})\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

(2.18)

Since
\vec{\Phi}
is a critical point of the energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
, it holds

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(\vec{B}\cdot\mathcal{\vec{W}}+\nabla_{j}V^{j})\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}=0.

(2.19)

As (
2.19
) holds on every small patch
\Sigma_{0}
of the manifold
\Sigma
, we can write

\vec{B}\cdot\mathcal{\vec{W}}+\nabla_{j}V^{j}=0.

The Euler-Lagrange equation for
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
corresponds to the normal variation of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
, that is
\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{\Phi}=\vec{B}
.
This gives

\mathcal{\vec{W}}=\vec{0}.

∎

Remark 2.2.5
.

We point out an important mistake of
[
35
]
(Proposition 5.6) where Robin-Graham and Reichert wrote the Willmore-Euler-Lagrange equation for
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
in an arbitrary codimension. They did not show their computation but followed a formalism which was used by Guven
[
37
]
to derive the energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
in codimension one.
Proposition 5.6 of
[
35
]
differs from (
2.1
) by two terms:
2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}((\vec{h}^{j}_{i}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})\vec{H})
and
-2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{i})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H})
.

Note that in codimension one, these two terms cancel out and Proposition 5.6 of
[
35
]
would then be correct.

We now prove an identity which holds on any 4-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
. This identity is obtained by varying

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{H^{2}}:=\int_{\Sigma_{0}\subset\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

(2.20)

on every small patch
\Sigma_{0}
of
\Sigma
and using the translation invariance of (
2.20
). Note that we are not considering the critical points of the energy (
2.20
). Hence the Willmore-type operator
\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{H^{2}}
for (
2.20
) does not satisfy

\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{H^{2}}=\vec{0}.

Lemma 2.2.6
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a smooth immersion of a 4-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
. Define

\displaystyle\vec{A}^{s}:=\nabla^{s}\vec{H}-2(|\vec{H}|^{2}g^{sk}-\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{sk})\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi}.

(2.21)

Then the following identity holds

\displaystyle\nabla_{s}\vec{A}^{s}=\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}-8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}.

Proof.

Using (
2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\delta|\vec{H}|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\vec{H}\cdot\delta\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\vec{H}\cdot\left(4A^{s}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}+\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B})\right).

Using (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle\quad\quad|g|^{-1/2}\delta\left(|\vec{H}|^{2}|g|^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle=2A^{s}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla_{s}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})+\frac{1}{2}\vec{H}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}+|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla_{s}A^{s}-4(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H})|\vec{H}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{s}(A^{s}|\vec{H}|^{2})+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})-4(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H})|\vec{H}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\nabla_{i}(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{B})-\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{B}\right]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{s}(A^{s}|\vec{H}|^{2})+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})-4(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H})|\vec{H}|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\frac{1}{2}\left[\nabla_{i}(\vec{H}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{B})-\nabla^{i}(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\cdot\vec{B})+\vec{B}\cdot\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{s}\left[A^{s}|\vec{H}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla^{s}\vec{B}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{s}\vec{H}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\vec{B}\cdot\left[\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}-4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{s}V_{H^{2}}^{s}+\vec{B}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{H^{2}}

where

V_{H^{2}}^{s}:=A^{s}|\vec{H}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla^{s}\vec{B}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{s}\vec{H}

and

\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{H^{2}}:=\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}-4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}.

We have on every small patch
\Sigma_{0}
the identity

\displaystyle\delta\int_{\Sigma_{0}}|\vec{H}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}=\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(\nabla_{s}V_{H^{2}}^{s}+\vec{B}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{H^{2}})\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

(2.22)

Recall
\delta\vec{\Phi}=A^{s}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}+\vec{B}
. Using invariance by translation of the energy (
2.20
):

\delta\vec{\Phi}=\vec{a},\,\,\mbox{where}\,\,\vec{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\,\,\mbox{is a constant vector}

yields

\displaystyle A^{s}=\vec{a}\cdot\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi}\,\quad\mbox{and}\,\quad\vec{B}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{a}.

(2.23)

Now, using (
2.23
) we have

\displaystyle V_{H^{2}}^{s}

\displaystyle=A^{s}|\vec{H}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{H}\cdot\nabla^{s}\vec{B}-\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{s}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\vec{a}\cdot\left[\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi}|\vec{H}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{s}\vec{H}-\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{s}\vec{H}\right]

\displaystyle=\vec{a}\cdot\left[\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi}|\vec{H}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{s}\vec{H}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{sk})\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi}\right]

(2.24)

so that equation (
2.22
) under translation invariance becomes

\displaystyle\delta\int_{\Sigma_{0}}|\vec{H}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}=\vec{a}\cdot\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(\nabla_{s}\vec{V}_{H^{2}}^{s}+\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{H^{2}})\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}=0

(2.25)

where
\vec{V}_{H^{2}}^{s}:=\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi}|\vec{H}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{s}\vec{H}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{sk})\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi}
. As (
2.25
) holds on every small patch
\Sigma_{0}
, we have
-\nabla_{s}\vec{V}_{H^{2}}^{s}=\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{H^{2}}
. That is,

\displaystyle-\nabla_{s}\left[\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi}|\vec{H}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{s}\vec{H}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{sk})\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}-4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}\right]

and then the desired identity follows.
∎

2.3
The other energies

The energy (
1.5
) is unbounded from below as observed by Robin-Graham and Reichert in
[
35
]
but we can add the energy
\mu\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
to it in order to obtain a nonnegative energy. This holds for
\mu\geq\frac{1}{12}
. In this section, we will investigate these other nonnegative energies in codimension one.

Proposition 2.3.1

(See Proposition 6.6 of
[
35
]
)
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{5}
be an immersion. If
\mu\geq\frac{1}{12}
, then the energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)+\mu\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
is nonnegative.

Proof.

By using the formula
(h_{0})_{ij}=h_{ij}-Hg_{ij}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)+\mu\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{4}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla H|^{2}-|H|^{2}(|h_{0}|^{2}+4|H|^{2})+7|H|^{2}+\mu|h_{0}|^{4}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla H|^{2}+3|H|^{4}-|H|^{2}|h_{0}|^{2}+\mu|h_{0}|^{4}.

Since
\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla H|^{2}\geq 0
, a sufficient condition for

\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)+\mu\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{4}
to be nonnegative is that the quadratic form
3x^{2}-xy+\mu y^{2}
be nonnegative definite. This gives that
3\mu-\frac{1}{4}\geq 0
which implies
\mu\geq\frac{1}{12}
.
∎

Remark 2.3.2
.

Among all energies of the form

\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)+\mu\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}\,,

we strongly suspect that the only energy whose critical points include minimal hypersurfaces is
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
.

We now give an argument to substantiate this remark. Since minimal hypersurfaces are critical points of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
, we only need to vary the energy
\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
and show that its critical points do not include minimal hypersurfaces.

By using the decomposition
(\vec{h}_{0})_{ij}=\vec{h}_{ij}-\vec{H}g_{ij}
we find that

\displaystyle|\vec{h}_{0}|^{4}=|\vec{h}|^{4}-8|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}+16|\vec{H}|^{4}.

The variations of
\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
and
\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
have already been done in the proof of Theorem (
2.2.2
) and clearly their critical points include minimal hypersurfaces. So, we only need to vary
\int_{\Sigma}|h|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
.

We find

\displaystyle\delta|\vec{h}|^{2}=\delta(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})=A^{s}\nabla_{s}(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})+4(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{is})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{s})+2\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B}.

Hence

\displaystyle\delta|\vec{h}|^{4}

\displaystyle=2|\vec{h}|^{2}\delta|\vec{h}|^{2}

\displaystyle=A^{s}\nabla_{s}|h|^{4}+8(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{is})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{s})|h|^{2}+4(\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B})|\vec{h}|^{2}

and

\displaystyle|g|^{-1/2}\delta(|\vec{h}|^{4}|g|^{1/2})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{s}(A^{s}|\vec{h}|^{4})+8(\vec{B}\cdot\vec{h}^{is})(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{s})|\vec{h}|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+4(\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\vec{B})|\vec{h}|^{2}-4|\vec{h}|^{4}\vec{B}\cdot\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{s}V_{3}^{s}+\vec{B}\cdot\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{3}

where

\displaystyle V_{3}^{s}

\displaystyle:=A^{s}|\vec{h}|^{4}+4(\vec{h}^{sj}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{B})|\vec{h}|^{2}-4\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{j}(\vec{h}^{js}|\vec{h}|^{2})

and

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{3}:=8\vec{h}^{is}(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{s})|\vec{h}|^{2}-4|\vec{h}|^{4}\vec{H}+4\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}(\vec{h}^{ij}|\vec{h}|^{2}).

(2.26)

The critical point of the energy
\int_{\Sigma}|h|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{0}}(\vec{B}\cdot\mathcal{\vec{W}}_{3}+\nabla_{j}V^{j}_{3})\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}=0.

(2.27)

As (
2.27
) holds on every small patch
\Sigma_{0}
of the manifold
\Sigma
, we can write

\vec{B}\cdot\mathcal{\vec{W}}_{3}+\nabla_{j}V^{j}_{3}=0.

The Euler-Lagrange equation for
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
corresponds to the normal variation of
\int_{\Sigma}|h|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}
, that is
\pi_{\vec{n}}\delta\vec{\Phi}=\vec{B}
.
This gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{\vec{W}}_{3}=\vec{0}.

(2.28)

We will now argue that minimal surfaces do not satisfy (
2.28
).
Assume that
\Sigma
is a minimal hypersurface.
The minimality assumption implies that

\displaystyle\vec{H}=\frac{1}{4}\vec{h}_{j}^{j}=\vec{0}.

(2.29)

By using (
2.29
) and the contracted Codazzi-Mainardi equation
(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{h}_{j}^{j}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\vec{h}_{i}^{j})
in (
2.26
), we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{\vec{W}}_{3}

\displaystyle=8\vec{h}^{is}(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{s})|\vec{h}|^{2}+4\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}(\vec{h}^{ij}|\vec{h}|^{2})

\displaystyle=4\vec{h}^{is}(\vec{h}_{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{s})|\vec{h}|^{2}+4\vec{h}^{ij}\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}|\vec{h}|^{2}.

(2.30)

It is hard to imagine that the assumption
\vec{H}=\vec{0}
would make the right hand side of (
2.30
) vanish. However, we do not rule out the possibility of having a minimal hypersurface for which the right hand side of (
2.30
) would vanish. Thus, we strongly suspect that for any
\mu
, the critical points of the energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)+\mu\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}

(2.31)

do not include minimal hypersurfaces.

Remark 2.3.3
.

One could also add another curvature term to have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)+\int_{\Sigma}\sigma\textnormal{Tr}(h_{0}^{4})+\mu|h_{0}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}\quad\quad\sigma,\mu\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.32)

However, computations show a strong evidence that minimal hypersurfaces are not critical points of the energies (
2.31
) and (
2.32
).

2.4
Conservation laws

In this section, the left hand side of the Willmore-type equation (
2.1
) is reformulated in terms of some divergence free quantities with the help of Noether’s theorem.

Let
\Omega
be an open subset of
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Let the Lagrangian

L:\{(x,y,z):(x,y)\in D\times E,\,z\in T_{y}E\otimes T^{*}_{x}D\}\mapsto\mathbb{R}

be continuously differentiable. By choosing a
C^{1}
density measure
d\mu(x)
on
\Omega
, we can define a functional
\mathcal{L}
on the set of maps
C^{1}(\Omega,E)
via

\mathcal{L}(u):=\int_{\Omega}L(x,u(x),du(x))\,d\mu(x).

A tangent vector
X
is an
infinitesimal symmetry
for
\mathcal{L}
if and only if

\frac{\partial L}{\partial y^{i}}(x,y,z)X^{i}(y)+\frac{\partial L}{\partial z^{i}_{\alpha}}(x,y,z)\frac{\partial X^{i}}{\partial y^{j}}(y)z^{j}_{\alpha}=0.

The vector field
J
defined by

J^{\alpha}:=\rho(x)\frac{\partial L}{\partial z^{i}_{\alpha}}(x,u,du)X^{i}(u)

where
\rho
satisfies
d\mu(x)=\rho(x)dx^{1}\cdots dx^{n}
, is often called
Noether current
in Physics. We are now ready to state a version of Noether’s theorem relevant for our purpose.

Theorem 2.4.1
.

Let
X
be a Lipschitz tangent vector field on
E
, which is an infinitesimal symmetry for
\mathcal{L}
. If
u:\Omega\rightarrow E
is a critical point of
\mathcal{L}
, then

\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{\partial J^{\alpha}}{\partial x^{\alpha}}=0

where
\{x^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1,...,n}
are coordinates on
\Omega
such that
d\mu(x)=\rho(x)dx^{1}\cdots dx^{n}
.

The following result holds by applying Noether’s theorem to the invariances of the energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma).

Theorem 2.4.2
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a smooth immersion of a 4-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
and let
\mathcal{\vec{W}}
be as defined in Proposition
2.2.2
with
\mathcal{\vec{W}}=\vec{0}
. Define the following quantity

\displaystyle\vec{V}^{j}:

\displaystyle=|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}-2(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H})\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{h}_{i}^{j}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H})\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}-2(\vec{h}_{i}^{j}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})\vec{H}+2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{i}^{j})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H}-2\nabla_{i}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H})

\displaystyle\quad+4\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H})-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle\quad+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})+7|\vec{H}|^{4}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+7\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})-14\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}).

Then the following conservation laws hold by the invariance of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
under translation, dilation and rotation respectively.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\vec{0}&=\nabla_{j}\vec{V}^{j}\\
0&=\nabla_{j}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}^{j}-\nabla^{j}|\vec{H}|^{2})\\
\vec{0}&=\nabla_{j}(\vec{\Phi}\wedge\vec{V}^{j}+\vec{M}^{j})\\
\end{array}\right.

where
\vec{M}^{j}
denotes the two-vector

\displaystyle\vec{M}^{j}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+2\vec{H}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}+2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik}\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}-7|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}.

Proof.

Translation.

Consider a translation
\delta\vec{\Phi}=\vec{a}
, where
\vec{a}
is a constant vector in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Then

A_{j}=\vec{a}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\vec{B}=\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{a}.

If
\vec{U}
is any vector, then it holds

\vec{U}\cdot\vec{B}=\vec{a}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

and

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}(\vec{B}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})-\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}(\vec{a}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})-\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{a}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

\displaystyle=\vec{a}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}-\vec{a}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

\displaystyle=\vec{a}\cdot\pi_{T}\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}.

Hence

\displaystyle\vec{U}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{B}=\nabla^{i}(\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})-(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{i}\left[\vec{a}\cdot\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right]-\vec{a}\cdot\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

\displaystyle=\vec{a}\cdot\left[\nabla^{i}\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}-\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right].

Then we have,

V^{j}=\vec{a}\cdot\vec{V}^{j},

where

\displaystyle\vec{V}^{j}

\displaystyle=|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{h}_{ik}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H})\vec{h}^{ik}-2(\vec{h}_{i}^{j}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})\vec{H}+2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{i}^{j})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}-\frac{1}{2}\pi_{T}\nabla^{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{i}\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}-\frac{1}{2}\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}

\displaystyle\quad-2\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H})+2\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H})-\frac{1}{2}\pi_{T}\nabla^{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+7|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+7\pi_{T}\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})

\displaystyle\quad-7\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})

\displaystyle=|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}-2(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{H})\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{h}_{i}^{j}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H})\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{j}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}

\displaystyle\quad-2(\vec{h}_{i}^{j}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{H})\vec{H}+2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{i}^{j})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H}-2\nabla_{i}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H})+4\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}((\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik})+7|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle\quad+7\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})-14\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}).

We note that

\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}^{j}=\Delta_{g}|\vec{H}|^{2}.

Dilation.

Next, we consider a dilation
\delta\vec{\Phi}=\lambda\vec{\Phi}
, for some constant
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Clearly,

A_{j}=\lambda\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\vec{B}=\lambda\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{\Phi}.

If
\vec{U}
is any vector, then it holds

\vec{U}\cdot\vec{B}=\lambda\vec{\Phi}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

and

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}(\vec{B}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})-\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}(\lambda\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{U})-\lambda\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

\displaystyle=\lambda\left(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}-\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda\vec{\Phi}\cdot\pi_{T}\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}.

Hence

\displaystyle\vec{U}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{B}=\nabla^{i}(\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})-(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{i}\left[\lambda\vec{\Phi}\cdot\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right]-\lambda\vec{\Phi}\cdot\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

\displaystyle=\lambda\vec{\Phi}\cdot\left[\nabla^{i}\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}-\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right]-4\lambda\vec{H}\cdot\vec{U}

From this and the computation above for translation, we find

V^{j}=\lambda\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}^{j}-2\lambda\vec{H}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{H}=\lambda\left[\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}^{j}-\nabla^{j}|\vec{H}|^{2}\right].

Rotation.
We now consider an infinitesimal rotation given by
3
3

3

Here, we use
\star
to denote the Hodge star operator on the ambient space.

\delta\vec{\Phi}=\star(\vec{b}\wedge\vec{\Phi})\quad\mbox{for some constant}\quad\vec{b}\in\Lambda^{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}).

Clearly,

A_{j}=\vec{b}\cdot\star(\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\vec{B}=(\vec{b}\cdot\star(\Phi\wedge\vec{n}_{\alpha}))\vec{n}_{\alpha}

where we have summed over
\alpha
.
If
\vec{U}
is any vector, then it holds that

\vec{U}\cdot\vec{B}=\vec{b}\cdot\star(\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})

and

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}(\vec{B}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})-\vec{B}\cdot\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}

\displaystyle=\vec{b}\cdot\star(\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{T}\nabla_{j}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}+\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}).

Hence

\displaystyle\vec{U}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{B}=\nabla^{i}(\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B})-(\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{i}\left(\vec{b}\cdot\star(\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}+\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\vec{b}\cdot\star\left(\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}+\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right)

\displaystyle=\vec{b}\cdot\star\left[\vec{\Phi}\wedge\left(\nabla^{i}\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}-\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right)+\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left.+\nabla^{i}(\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U})-\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right]

\displaystyle=\vec{b}\cdot\star\left[\vec{\Phi}\wedge\left(\nabla^{i}\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}-\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right)+2\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}+4\vec{H}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right]

\displaystyle=\vec{b}\cdot\star\left[\vec{\Phi}\wedge\left(\nabla^{i}\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}-\pi_{T}\nabla_{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right)+4\vec{H}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{U}\right].

From this and the computation above for translation, we find

\displaystyle V^{j}

\displaystyle=\vec{b}\cdot\star\left[\vec{\Phi}\wedge\vec{V}^{j}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+2\vec{H}\wedge\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{j}\vec{H}+2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left.+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})\vec{h}_{ik}\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}-7|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}\right].

∎

2.5
Conformal invariance

The goal of this Section is to prove the following Theorem. We adopt the techniques found in
[
37
]
in our proof. We assume that
\Sigma
is a closed manifold so that boundary terms vanish whenever we integrate by parts.

Theorem 2.5.1
.

The energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
is conformally invariant.

Proof.

Consider the energy

\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\int_{\Sigma}|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}d\text{vol}_{g}.

Since it is invariant under reparametrisation, only normal variations matter (tangential variations contribute to terms that appear as exact derivatives and can be integrated away). Moreover, as we have seen by using Noether’s theorem and the invariance under translation, critical points of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
satisfy an equation of the type

\nabla_{j}\vec{F}^{j}=\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{0},

for some suitable vector field
\vec{F}^{j}
and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
is a normal vector. Actually, we can identify
\vec{F}^{j}
as the component of the stress-energy tensor associated with
\mathcal{E}_{0}
.
Accordingly, for a variation
\delta\vec{\Phi}
, we have

-\delta\mathcal{E}_{0}=-\int_{\Sigma}\vec{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\cdot\delta\vec{\Phi}=-\int_{\Sigma}\nabla_{j}\vec{F}^{j}\cdot\delta\vec{\Phi}=\int_{\Sigma}\vec{F}^{j}\cdot\nabla_{j}\delta\vec{\Phi}.

Let us consider a special conformal variation of the type

\delta\vec{\Phi}=|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\vec{\Phi})\vec{\Phi}

where
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a constant vector.
We see that
\vec{F}^{j}
satisfies

\vec{F}^{j}=F^{ij}\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{G}^{ij}+C^{j}\vec{H}

where

F^{ij}=|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}g^{ij}-2\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla^{i}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H},

\vec{G}^{ij}=2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}g^{ij}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}

and

C^{j}=-4\nabla^{j}|\vec{H}|^{2}-4\vec{h}^{jk}\cdot\nabla_{k}\vec{H}=4\nabla_{k}(|\vec{H}|^{2}g^{jk}-\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk}).

Let us note that if
\vec{u}^{j}
is a normal vector, then

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot\nabla_{j}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\vec{\Phi})\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\nabla_{i}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{j}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\vec{\Phi})\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\nabla_{i}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\pi_{\vec{n}}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\nabla_{i}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\vec{\Phi})-\int_{\Sigma}\nabla_{i}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\pi_{T}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\pi_{T}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\nabla_{i}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\vec{\Phi})+\int_{\Sigma}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot\nabla_{i}(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\pi_{T}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\pi_{T}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\nabla_{i}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\vec{\Phi})+\int_{\Sigma}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\pi_{T}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\pi_{T}\vec{\Phi}).

But

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\pi_{T}\vec{c}=(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi})\vec{h}_{i}^{k}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\pi_{T}\vec{\Phi}=(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi})\vec{h}_{i}^{k}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{k}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{h}_{i}^{k}.

Hence, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\vec{u}^{ij}\cdot(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\pi_{T}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\pi_{T}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{h}^{k}_{i}\cdot\vec{u}^{ij})\left(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi})-\nabla_{k}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\right).

This is in particular the case for
\vec{u}^{ij}=\vec{G}^{ij}
which we will adopt later.

Now, we have

\vec{F}^{j}\cdot\nabla_{j}\delta\vec{\Phi}=(F^{ij}\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla_{i}\vec{G}^{ij}+C^{j}\vec{H})\cdot\nabla_{j}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\vec{\Phi})\vec{\Phi}).

Upon integration by parts, the latter yields
4
4

4

See page
2.1
for the definition of the interior multiplication
{{{{\llcorner}}}}
.

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{0}

\displaystyle=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\vec{G}^{j}_{j}-(\vec{G}^{ij}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+C^{j}(\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\int_{\Sigma}F^{ij}\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{j}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}\vec{c}-2(\vec{c}\cdot\vec{\Phi})\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{h}^{k}_{i}\cdot\vec{G}^{ij})\left(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi})-\nabla_{k}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\right)

\displaystyle=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\vec{G}^{j}_{j}+F^{j}_{j}\vec{\Phi}-(\vec{G}^{ij}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+C^{j}(\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\int_{\Sigma}F^{ij}\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})-F^{ij}\nabla_{i}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{h}^{k}_{i}\cdot\vec{G}^{ij})\left(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi})-\nabla_{k}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})\right)

\displaystyle=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\vec{G}^{j}_{j}+F^{j}_{j}\vec{\Phi}-(\vec{G}^{ij}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+C^{j}(\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}
