\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\int_{\Sigma}(F^{kj}+\vec{h}^{k}_{i}\cdot\vec{G}^{ij})(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi})-\nabla_{k}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})).

(2.33)

On the other hand, we have

F^{kj}+\vec{h}^{k}_{i}\cdot\vec{G}^{ij}\quad\mbox{is symmetric}

so that

\displaystyle(F^{kj}+\vec{h}^{k}_{i}\cdot\vec{G}^{ij})(\nabla_{j}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi})-\nabla_{k}|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}(\vec{c}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}))=0.

(2.34)

Also,

\vec{G}^{j}_{j}=2\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H},\quad\quad F^{j}_{j}=2|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}+2\vec{H}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}=\Delta_{g}|\vec{H}|^{2}

so that

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}F^{j}_{j}\vec{\Phi}=4\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}.

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\vec{G}^{j}_{j}+F^{j}_{j}\vec{\Phi}=\int_{\Sigma}28|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}-2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij},

(2.35)

where we have used the following identity derived in Lemma
2.2.6

\displaystyle\nabla^{s}\vec{A}_{s}:=\nabla^{s}\left[\nabla_{s}\vec{H}-2(|\vec{H}|^{2}g_{sk}-\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{sk})\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}\right]=\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij}-8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}.

(2.36)

Next, integrating by parts, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}C^{j}(\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}=4\int_{\Sigma}\nabla_{k}(|\vec{H}|^{2}g^{jk}-\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})(\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=-4\int_{\Sigma}(|\vec{H}|^{2}g^{jk}-\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})\left[(\nabla_{k}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{H}\wedge\vec{h}_{jk}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi}\right]

\displaystyle=-4\int_{\Sigma}(|\vec{H}|^{2}g^{jk}-\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})\left[(\nabla_{k}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{H}\wedge\vec{h}_{jk}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}-\vec{H}g_{jk}\right]

\displaystyle=-4\int_{\Sigma}(|\vec{H}|^{2}g^{jk}-\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})\left[(\nabla_{k}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{H}\wedge\vec{h}_{jk}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}\right]

\displaystyle=4\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})\left[(\nabla_{k}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{H}\wedge\vec{h}_{jk}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}\right]-4\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}(\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}.

(2.37)

On the other hand, using again (
2.36
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{G}^{ij}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}=2\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})(\vec{H}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}-2\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{H}\wedge\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=2\int_{\Sigma}\left[(\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}-(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij})\wedge\vec{H}\right]{{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=2\int_{\Sigma}\left[(\nabla^{s}\vec{A}_{s}-2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})\vec{h}^{ij})\wedge\vec{H}\right]{{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=-4\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})(\vec{h}^{ij}\wedge\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}-2\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{A}^{s}\wedge\nabla_{s}\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{A}^{s}\wedge\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=-4\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})(\vec{h}^{ij}\wedge\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}-2\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{A}^{s}\wedge\nabla_{s}\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+(\vec{A}^{s}\cdot\nabla_{s}\vec{\Phi})\vec{H}

\displaystyle=-4\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})(\vec{h}^{ij}\wedge\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}-2\int_{\Sigma}(\vec{A}^{s}\wedge\nabla_{s}\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}-4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}-4(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}_{ij})(\vec{h}^{ij}\wedge\vec{H}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}-4|\vec{H}|^{2}(\nabla^{j}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Sigma}4(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})(\nabla_{k}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}.

(2.38)

We find from (
2.37
) and (
2.38
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}C^{j}(\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}-(\vec{G}^{ij}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}=-8\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}.

(2.39)

Using (
2.34
), (
2.35
) and (
2.39
) in (
2.33
) gives

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{0}=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}20|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}-2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}.

Consider next the energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

Using similar notation as above, we have this time

\displaystyle F^{ij}=|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}g^{ij}-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{kl})(\vec{h}_{kl}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})-2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ik})(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{j}_{k})

and

\displaystyle\vec{G}^{ij}=-2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{H}-\frac{1}{2}g^{ij}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{kl})\vec{h}_{kl}\quad\quad\textnormal{and}\quad C^{j}=0.

(2.40)

By same token as above

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{1}

\displaystyle=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\vec{G}^{j}_{j}+F^{j}_{j}\vec{\Phi}-(\vec{G}^{ij}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\vec{G}^{j}_{j}

\displaystyle=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}-8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}-2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}

Finally, we consider the energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}:=\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{4}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

Using similar notation as above, we have

\displaystyle F^{ij}=|\vec{H}|^{4}g^{ij}-|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij},

\displaystyle\vec{G}^{ij}=-|\vec{H}^{2}|\vec{H}g^{ij}\quad\quad\textnormal{and}\quad C^{j}=0.

By same token as above, we find

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\vec{G}^{j}_{j}+F^{j}_{j}\vec{\Phi}-(\vec{G}^{ij}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij}){{{{\llcorner}}}}\vec{\Phi}=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\vec{G}^{j}_{j}

\displaystyle=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}-4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}:=\delta(\mathcal{E}_{0}+a\mathcal{E}_{1}+b\mathcal{E}_{2})=-2\vec{c}\cdot\int_{\Sigma}(-2a-2)(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}+(20-8a-4b)|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}

so that only the values
a=-1
and
b=7
yields
\delta\mathcal{E}=0
.

Observe that we have just shown that the only linear combination of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
,
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
that gives a conformally invariant energy is

\int_{\Sigma}|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}+7|\vec{H}|^{4}.

This completes the proof.
∎

2.6
Existence of potential 2-forms

In this section, we present further conservation laws. We briefly clarify some of the notations used in this Section.
Most of the quantities used are simultaneoulsy differential forms and multivectors. The space
\Lambda^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
is the collection of all
k
-forms that act as
p
-vectors from
\mathbb{R}^{4}
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Thus an element of this space is both a
p
-vector and a
k
-form, or simply a
p
-vector-valued
k
-form. For
\vec{A}\in\Lambda^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
and
\vec{B}\in\Lambda^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
, the product
\vec{A}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{B}\in\Lambda^{k+\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{p+q-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
represents the contraction between multivectors and wedge product between forms. Similar meaning is given to the products
\vec{A}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{B}
and
\vec{A}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{B}
where the upper and lower symbols denote operations between vectors and forms respectively. We will simply write
\vec{A}\wedge\vec{B}
whenever it is clear to do so. For instance, if
\vec{A}
is a 1-vector-valued 0-form and
\vec{B}
is a
q
-vector valued
\ell
-form.

We recall the following useful result due to Henri Poincaré (see
[
43
]
).

Lemma 2.6.1

(Poincaré lemma)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be an open ball. Let
\omega\in\Lambda^{k}(\Omega,\Lambda^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
with
1\leq k\leq 4
satisfy

d\omega=0.

Then
\omega=d\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\Lambda^{k-1}(\Omega,\Lambda^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
. Similarly, if
0\leq k\leq 3
and

d^{\star}\omega=0,

then
\omega=d^{\star}\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\Lambda^{k+1}(\Omega,\Lambda^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
.

Proposition 2.6.2
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}
be a critical point of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
and let
\vec{V}
be as defined in Proposition
2.4.2
. Then there is a two-form
\vec{l}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
such that

\displaystyle\vec{V}=\star d\vec{l}_{0}.

Moreover, there are two-forms
X\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
and
\vec{Y}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
such that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}dX&=\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}-\star d|\vec{H}|^{2}\\
d\vec{Y}&=\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}-\star\vec{M}\end{cases}

where
\star
is the usual Hodge star operator on differential forms.

Proof.

From Proposition
2.4.2
, it holds that
\nabla_{j}\vec{V}^{j}=\vec{0}.
In other words,
d^{\star}\vec{V}=\vec{0}
where
\vec{V}\in\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
. By the Poincaré lemma, there exists some
\vec{l}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
such that

\displaystyle\vec{V}=\star d\vec{l}_{0}\quad\quad\mbox{with}\quad d^{\star}\vec{l}_{0}=\vec{0}.

Similarly, we found in Proposition
2.4.2
,

d^{\star}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}-d|\vec{H}|^{2})=\nabla_{j}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}-\nabla^{j}|\vec{H}|^{2})=0.

By the Poincaré lemma, there is some
X_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
such that

\displaystyle d^{\star}X_{0}

\displaystyle=\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}-d|\vec{H}|^{2}=\star d\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{\Phi}-d|\vec{H}|^{2}.

We are free to demand that
dX_{0}=-\star\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}
. Thus we have

\displaystyle d\star X_{0}=-d\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{\Phi}-\star d|\vec{H}|^{2}=-d(\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{\Phi})+\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}-\star d|\vec{H}|^{2}

By setting
X:=\star X_{0}+\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{\Phi}
where
X\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
, we have that

\displaystyle dX=\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}-\star d|\vec{H}|^{2}\quad\quad\mbox{with}\quad d^{\star}X=0.

Finally, recall that in Proposition
2.4.2
we obtained
d^{\star}(\vec{\Phi}\wedge\vec{V}+\vec{M})=\vec{0}
. By the Poincaré lemma, there exists some
\vec{Y}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
such that

\displaystyle d^{\star}\vec{Y}_{0}

\displaystyle=\vec{\Phi}\wedge\vec{V}+\vec{M}=\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}\star d\vec{l}_{0}+\vec{M}.

We are free to demand that
d\vec{Y}_{0}=\star\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}
. Thus we have

\displaystyle d\star\vec{Y}_{0}=-\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{l}_{0}+\star\vec{M}=-d(\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{l}_{0})+d\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{l}_{0}+\star\vec{M}.

By defining a two-vector
\vec{Y}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
by
\vec{Y}:=-\star\vec{Y}_{0}-\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}\vec{l}_{0},
we have

\displaystyle d\vec{Y}=\vec{l}_{0}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}-\star\vec{M}\quad\quad\mbox{with}\quad d^{\star}\vec{Y}=\vec{0}.

∎

For legibility and simplicity of notations, we now present further conservation laws in codimension one. From now on,
\Sigma
will be understood as a four dimensional closed hypersurface.

Proposition 2.6.3
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{5}
be a critical point of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
and let
\vec{V}
be as defined in Theorem
2.4.2
.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle d^{\star}\vec{V}=\vec{0},

\displaystyle d^{\star}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}-d|\vec{H}|^{2})=0

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad d^{\star}(\vec{\Phi}\wedge\vec{V}+(\vec{J}+2d^{\star}(|\vec{H}|^{2}d\vec{\Phi}))\wedge d\vec{\Phi})=\vec{0}

where
\vec{J}:=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}\vec{H}|\vec{h}|^{2}-7|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}
.
Also, there exist two-forms
\vec{L}_{0},\vec{L}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
,
S_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
and
\vec{R}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
such that

\displaystyle\vec{V}=d^{\star}\vec{L}_{0}

\displaystyle dS_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle d\vec{R}_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}+\star d\vec{u}-(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}

where where
\vec{L}_{0}
and
\vec{L}
are related by
\vec{L}=\star(\vec{L}_{0}-d|\vec{H}|^{2}\wedge_{4}d\vec{\Phi})
and
\vec{u}
satisfies the Hodge decomposition

\displaystyle\frac{5}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}=d\vec{u}+d^{\star}\vec{v},

\displaystyle\quad\vec{u}\in\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5})),\vec{v}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5})),\vec{\eta}:=d\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5})).

Proof.

From Theorem
2.4.2
, we have
\nabla_{j}\vec{V}^{j}=\vec{0}.
In other words,
d^{\star}\vec{V}=\vec{0}
where
\vec{V}\in\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
. By the Poincaré lemma, there exists some
\vec{L}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
such that

\displaystyle\vec{V}=d^{\star}\vec{L}_{0}

(2.41)

with the choice

d\vec{L}_{0}=\vec{0}.

Also, from Theorem
2.4.2
, the invariance of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
under dilation implies that

\displaystyle d^{\star}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{V}-d|\vec{H}|^{2})=\nabla_{j}(\vec{V}^{j}\cdot\vec{\Phi}-\nabla^{j}|\vec{H}|^{2})=0.

(2.42)

Note that

\displaystyle\vec{V}\cdot\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=d^{\star}\vec{L}_{0}\cdot\vec{\Phi}=\star d\star\vec{L}_{0}\cdot\vec{\Phi}=\star\left(d(\star\vec{L}_{0}\cdot\vec{\Phi})-(\star\vec{L}_{0})\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\right)

\displaystyle=d^{\star}(\vec{L}_{0}\cdot\vec{\Phi})-\star((\star\vec{L}_{0})\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})

so that (
2.42
) becomes

\displaystyle d^{\star}\left[\star((\star\vec{L}_{0})\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})+d|\vec{H}|^{2}\right]=0

or equivalently

\displaystyle d\left[(\star\vec{L}_{0})\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}-\star d|\vec{H}|^{2}\right]=0.

Hence, there exists
S_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
such that

\displaystyle dS_{0}=(\star\vec{L}_{0})\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}-\star d|\vec{H}|^{2}.

Further computations show that

\displaystyle dS_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}(\star\vec{L}_{0})_{[kl}\cdot\nabla_{m]}\vec{\Phi}\,\,\,dx^{k}\wedge_{4}dx^{l}\wedge_{4}dx^{m}-\frac{1}{6}\epsilon_{iklm}\nabla^{i}|\vec{H}|^{2}\,\,\,dx^{k}\wedge_{4}dx^{l}\wedge_{4}dx^{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\epsilon_{ij[kl}\nabla_{m]}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\vec{L}_{0}^{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{[i}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j]}\vec{\Phi}\right)\,\,\,dx^{k}\wedge_{4}dx^{l}\wedge_{4}dx^{m}

\displaystyle=\star(\vec{L}_{0}-d|\vec{H}|^{2}\wedge_{4}d\vec{\Phi})\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}.

By setting

\vec{L}:=\star(\vec{L}_{0}-d|\vec{H}|^{2}\wedge_{4}d\vec{\Phi})

we find that

\displaystyle dS_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}.

Note that
d^{\star}\vec{L}=\star d\vec{L}_{0}=\vec{0}.

Now, it holds in codimension one that

\displaystyle\vec{V}^{j}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}(\vec{J}\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+2|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{h}^{ij}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}(\vec{J}\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+2\nabla^{i}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi})

so that

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\left(\vec{V}^{j}\wedge\vec{\Phi}-(\vec{J}+2\nabla^{i}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})\wedge\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi})\right)=\vec{0}

or equivalently

\displaystyle d^{\star}\left(\vec{V}\wedge\vec{\Phi}-\vec{J}\wedge d\vec{\Phi}-2d^{\star}(|\vec{H}|^{2}d\vec{\Phi})\wedge d\vec{\Phi}\right)=\vec{0}.

(2.43)

Observe that

\displaystyle\vec{V}\wedge\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=d^{\star}\vec{L}_{0}\wedge\vec{\Phi}=\star d\star\vec{L}_{0}\wedge\vec{\Phi}=\star\left[d(\star\vec{L}_{0}\wedge\vec{\Phi})-(\star\vec{L}_{0})\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\right]

\displaystyle=d^{\star}(\vec{L}_{0}\wedge\vec{\Phi})-\star\left[(\star\vec{L}_{0})\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\right]

\displaystyle=d^{\star}(\vec{L}_{0}\wedge\vec{\Phi})-\star(\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})-\star\left[\left(\star(d|\vec{H}|^{2}\wedge_{4}d\vec{\Phi})\right)\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\right].

(2.44)

We will choose
\vec{\eta}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
as follows

\displaystyle\vec{\eta}=\frac{1}{2}\vec{\eta}_{ij}\,\,\,dx^{i}\wedge_{4}dx^{j}:=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}\,\,\,dx^{i}\wedge_{4}dx^{j}.

(2.45)

Focusing on (
2.44
), we see that

\displaystyle\star\left[\left(\star(d|\vec{H}|^{2}\wedge_{4}d\vec{\Phi})\right)\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\epsilon^{klmr}\left(\frac{1}{6}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{klm}\epsilon_{ij\alpha\beta}\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{[i}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j]}\vec{\Phi}\right)\wedge\nabla_{\gamma}\vec{\Phi}\,\,g_{rs}\,\,\,dx^{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\delta_{ij}^{mr}\nabla^{[i}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j]}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}g_{rs}dx^{s}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{m}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{m}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{r}\vec{\Phi}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{m}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{m}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{r}\vec{\Phi})dx^{r}+\frac{1}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla_{m}(\nabla^{m}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{r}\vec{\Phi})dx^{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}d^{\star}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{\eta})+\frac{1}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}.

Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle\vec{V}\wedge\vec{\Phi}=d^{\star}(\vec{L}_{0}\wedge\vec{\Phi})-\star(\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})+\frac{1}{3}d^{\star}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{\eta})-\frac{1}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}

and so (
2.43
) becomes

\displaystyle d^{\star}\left[-\star(\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})-\frac{1}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}-\vec{J}\wedge d\vec{\Phi}-2d^{\star}(|\vec{H}|^{2}d\vec{\Phi})\wedge d\vec{\Phi}\right]=\vec{0}.

(2.46)

Observe that

\displaystyle d^{\star}(|\vec{H}|^{2}d\vec{\Phi})\wedge d\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi})\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}dx^{i}

\displaystyle=4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}dx^{i}+\nabla_{j}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}dx^{i}

\displaystyle=4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}dx^{i}+\nabla^{j}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})dx^{i}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}(\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})dx^{i}

\displaystyle=4|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\wedge d\vec{\Phi}+d^{\star}(|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{\eta})-|\vec{H}|^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}

(2.47)

Now, substitute (
2.47
) into (
2.46
) to find

\displaystyle d^{\star}\left[-\star(\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})+\frac{5}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}-(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})\wedge d\vec{\Phi}\right]=\vec{0}.

We introduce the Hodge decomposition

\displaystyle\frac{5}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}=d\vec{u}+d^{\star}\vec{v}

so that

\displaystyle d^{\star}\left[-\star(\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})+d\vec{u}-(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})\wedge d\vec{\Phi}\right]=\vec{0}

or equivalently

\displaystyle d\left[\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}+\star d\vec{u}-(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}\right]=\vec{0}.

(2.48)

Therefore there exists
\vec{R}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
such that

\displaystyle d\vec{R}_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}+\star d\vec{u}-(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}.

This completes the proof.

∎

Remark 2.6.4
.

We have proved in Theorem
2.6.3
the existence of 2-forms
\vec{L}
,
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
. Observe that
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
are defined in terms of
\vec{L}
which depends on some geometric quantity
\vec{V}
. The following computations show that
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
can be directly linked back to some geometric quantities via the “return equation”.

Return equation

\displaystyle\quad\quad\epsilon^{mijk}\nabla_{i}(\vec{R}_{0})_{jk}\bullet\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{mijk}\left[\vec{L}_{jk}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}+\epsilon_{rijk}\nabla^{r}\vec{u}-\epsilon_{rijk}(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})\wedge\nabla^{r}\vec{\Phi}\right]\bullet\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{mijk}(\vec{L}_{jk}\cdot\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi})\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}+6\delta^{m}_{r}\nabla^{r}\vec{u}\bullet\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}-6\delta^{m}_{r}(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})(-\delta^{r}_{m})

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{mijk}\nabla_{m}(S_{0})_{jk}\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}+6\nabla_{i}\vec{u}\bullet\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}+24(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}).

We have found

\displaystyle\epsilon^{mijk}(\nabla_{i}(\vec{R}_{0})_{jk}\bullet\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}+\nabla_{i}(S_{0})_{jk}\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi})-6\nabla_{i}\vec{u}\bullet\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=24(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})

(2.49)

This last equation (
2.49
) can be further simplified in order to re-introduce
v
.

Observe that

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\vec{u}\bullet\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{5}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla_{j}(\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})-\nabla^{j}\vec{v}_{ji}\right]\bullet\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}\left(4\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}+\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\vec{h}_{ji}\right)\bullet\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}-\nabla_{j}\vec{v}^{ji}\bullet\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}\left(-16\vec{H}+4\vec{H}\right)-\nabla_{j}\vec{v}^{ji}\bullet\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=-20|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}-\nabla_{j}\vec{v}^{ji}\bullet\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}.

Thus we find that

\displaystyle\epsilon^{mijk}\nabla_{i}(\vec{R}_{0})_{jk}\bullet\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}+\epsilon^{mijk}\nabla_{i}(S_{0})_{jk}\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}+6\nabla_{j}\vec{v}^{ji}\bullet\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}=24(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})-120|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}.

Clearly,

\displaystyle 24(\vec{J}+8|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H})-120|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=24\left(\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}+|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}\right)-120|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=12\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+12(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{ij})\vec{h}_{ij}-96|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}

\displaystyle=12\nabla_{j}\left[\nabla^{j}\vec{H}-2|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi}\right]

(2.50)

where we have used Lemma
2.2.6
to write (
2.50
).
Hence, we have found

\displaystyle\epsilon^{mijk}\nabla_{i}(\vec{R}_{0})_{jk}\bullet\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}+\epsilon^{mijk}\nabla_{i}(S_{0})_{jk}\nabla_{m}\vec{\Phi}+6\nabla_{j}\vec{v}^{ji}\bullet\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=12\nabla_{j}\left[\nabla^{j}\vec{H}-2|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+2(\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}^{jk})\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi}\right].

(2.51)

Lastly, applying divergence to the Hodge decomposition

\nabla^{j}\vec{u}+\nabla_{i}\vec{v}^{ij}=\frac{5}{3}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla_{i}\vec{\eta}^{ij}

yields

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}\vec{u}=\frac{5}{3}\nabla_{j}|\vec{H}|^{2}\nabla_{i}\vec{\eta}^{ij}.

(2.52)

Also,
\vec{v}
satisfies the equation

\Delta\vec{v}=\frac{5}{3}d\left(|\vec{H}|^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\right).

Chapter 3
Regularity Results

We prove that the critical point of the 4-dimensional Willmore-type energy (
3.1
) is smooth. In particular, we have

Theorem 3.0.1
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}\in W^{3,2}\cap W^{1,\infty}
be a non-degenerate critical point of the energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\Sigma):=\int_{\Sigma}\left(|\pi_{\vec{n}}\nabla\vec{H}|^{2}-|\vec{H}\cdot\vec{h}|^{2}+7|\vec{H}|^{2}\right)d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

(3.1)

Then
\vec{\Phi}
is smooth with the estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert DH\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}+\frac{1}{r}\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\left\lVert DH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{1})}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{1})}\right)\quad\quad\forall\,r<1

where
B_{r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
is any ball of radius
r
.

3.1
Introduction and outline

The proof of Theorem
3.0.1
is divided into different sections of this Chapter. In Section
3.2
, we develop generic regularity results applicable to our problem. The goal of Section
3.3
is to understand the regularity of the quantities
\vec{V},\vec{L}_{0},\vec{L},S_{0},\vec{R}_{0}
and
\vec{u}
obtained in Chapter
2
. A major achievement of this section is the existence of an invertible operator
\vec{\mathcal{P}}
used in creating new two forms:
\vec{L}_{1},S
and
\vec{R}
which are estimated in Section
3.4
. Section
3.5
answers the important question of how to link the regularity of the primitives
S
and
\vec{R}
back to geometric quantities like
\vec{H}
or
\vec{\Phi}
. This is done via the “return equation” obtained in Chapter
2
. By obtaining a Morrey-type estimate, the integrability of
d\vec{H}
is improved, the bootstrap argument ensues and the proof is finished.

It is important to clarify some of the notations used in subsequent sections. The operators
d
and
d^{\star}
are the well known exterior differential and codifferential, respectively, of differential forms. They are defined in terms of the metric induced Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
.   The operator
D
involves the flat partial derivatives. In the sequel, the sign
\lesssim
will indicate the presence of an unimportant multiplicative constant depending on
||\vec{\Phi}||_{W^{3,2}\cap W^{1,\infty}}
.

3.2
Preliminary results

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a ball. The Sobolev space
W^{k,p}(\Omega)
of measurable functions from
\Omega
into
\mathbb{R}^{m}
is defined by

W^{k,p}(\Omega):=\left\{f\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,:\,D^{\alpha}f\in L^{p}(\Omega)\,\,\forall\,\,\alpha\,\,\mbox{with}\,\,|\alpha|\leq k\right\}

with the norm

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{k,p}(\Omega)}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\left\lVert D^{\alpha}f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

The dual of
W^{k,p}(\Omega)
is
W^{-k,p}(\Omega)
.

The homogeneous Sobolev space
\dot{W}^{k,p}(\Omega)
is the space of all
k
-weakly differentiable functions
f\in L^{p}(\Omega)
such that
D^{\alpha}f\in L^{p}(\Omega)
for all
|\alpha|=k
.

The closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
W^{k,p}(\Omega)
is denoted by
W_{0}^{k,p}(\Omega)
.

Suppose that
\vec{\Phi}\in W^{3,2}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
is a critical point of the energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma).

By hypothesis, the coefficients of the metric tensor given by
g_{ij}:=\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}
lie in the space
W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
. We are interested in the regularity of Willmore-type manifolds with no branch point, that is the metric does not degenerate. The metric coefficients are uniformly bounded from above and below and satisfy, in a local coordinate chart
\Omega
of
\Sigma
:

\displaystyle g_{ij}\simeq\delta_{ij}\quad\quad\textnormal{as (4 $\times$ 4)-matrices.}

In other words,
g_{ij}
satisfies on
\Omega

\displaystyle c^{-1}\delta_{ij}u^{i}v^{j}\leq g_{ij}u^{i}v^{j}\leq c\delta_{ij}u^{i}v^{j}

for some
c>0
and for all
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. We have used
\delta_{ij}
to denote the
(i,j)-
component of the flat metric.

For
A\in\Lambda^{k}(\Omega)
and
B\in\Lambda^{k}(\Omega)
with local representations

A=\frac{1}{k!}A_{[i_{1}....i_{k}]}dx^{i_{1}}\wedge_{4}\cdots\wedge_{4}dx^{i_{k}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad B=\frac{1}{k!}B_{[j_{1}....j_{k}]}dx^{j_{1}}\wedge_{4}\cdots\wedge_{4}dx^{j_{k}}

we define the usual inner product on
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}^{4}

\displaystyle\langle A,B\rangle:=\frac{1}{(2k)!}\int_{\Omega}g^{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots g^{i_{k}j_{k}}A_{[i_{1}...i_{k}}B_{j_{1}...j_{k}]}dx^{1}\wedge_{4}\cdots\wedge_{4}dx^{4}.

Since the metric is controlled by the Euclidean metric on
\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\langle A,B\rangle\simeq\langle A,B\rangle_{0}:=\frac{1}{(2k)!}\int_{\Omega}\delta^{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots\delta^{i_{k}j_{k}}A_{[i_{1}...i_{k}}B_{j_{1}...j_{k}]}dx^{1}\wedge_{4}\cdots\wedge_{4}dx^{4}

which is the usual inner-product on forms for the flat metric
\delta
. Accordingly, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{L^{p}}\equiv\sup_{\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}}=1}\langle A,B\rangle_{0}=\sup_{\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}}}=1}\langle A,B\rangle\quad\quad\forall\,\,p\in[1,\infty)\,,\,\,\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1.

This fact will be used recurrently and tacitly in subsequent sections, in particular, when calling upon duality arguments.

First, we discuss the regularising properties of an equation of the type

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Delta u=f&\mbox{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\,\,\,\,\,u=0&\mbox{on}\,\,\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

(3.2)

Equivalently, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[u]:=\partial_{i}(|g|^{1/2}g^{ij}\partial_{j}u)=|g|^{1/2}f

where
\mathcal{L}
is a second-order partial differential operator. The coefficient
a^{ij}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by
a^{ij}:=|g|^{1/2}g^{ij}
is clearly symmetric and
satisfies the uniform ellipticity condition: there exists some
c>0
such that

\displaystyle c^{-1}\delta^{ij}u_{i}v_{j}\leq a^{ij}u_{i}v_{j}\leq c\delta^{ij}u_{i}v_{j}\,,\,\,\forall\,\,u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{4}.

Unfortunately, the coefficients
a^{ij}
are not Hölder continuous and standard regularity theory tools for elliptic equations cannot be applied but thanks to Claim
3.2.2
and Proposition
3.2.5
, we are still able to obtain the desired results.

Claim 3.2.2
.

The coefficients
a^{ij}
belong to the space
W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
.

Proof.

First, we show that
W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
is an algebra under pointwise multiplication. Let
\alpha,\beta\in W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
. Clearly,
\alpha\beta\in L^{\infty}
. It remains to show that
\alpha\beta\in W^{2,2}
. By Liebnitz rule we write

\displaystyle D^{2}(\alpha\beta)=\alpha D^{2}\beta+\beta D^{2}\alpha+2D\alpha D\beta.

Now,
\alpha\in L^{\infty},D^{2}\beta\in L^{2}
implies that
\alpha D^{2}\beta\in L^{2}
. By the same token
\beta D^{2}\alpha\in L^{2}
. By Sobolev embedding, the functions
D\alpha,D\beta\in W^{1,2}\subset L^{4}
so that the product
D\alpha D\beta\in L^{2}
. We arrive at
\alpha\beta\in W^{2,2}
. Thus
\alpha\beta\in W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
.

Next, using the hypothesis
\vec{\Phi}\in W^{3,2}\cap W^{1,\infty}
and the fact that
W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
is an algebra under pointwise multiplication, we have

g^{ij}:=\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}\in W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
. Hence
a^{ij}\in W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
.
∎

By the Sobolev embedding theorem,
W^{2,2}\subset W^{1,4}
. Thus
a^{ij}
belong to
L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,4}
.
It is important to note the role of the fact that
g^{ij}\in L^{\infty}
in the proof of Claim
3.2.2
(as
W^{2,2}
is not an algebra under pointwise multiplication). Otherwise, it would be difficult to now arrive at
a^{ij}\in W^{1,4}
. This is much needed because uniformly elliptic operators with coefficients in
W^{1,4}
have fairly good dispositions towards regularity theory.

Remark 3.2.3
.

Although it is true that the product of a function in
L^{p}
and any metric component or its determinant remains in
L^{p}
, the same cannot be said of Sobolev spaces (or of their duals). This is because the metric coefficients are not sufficiently differentiable (not Lipschitz). But the coefficients belong to
L^{\infty}\cap W^{2,2}
by Claim
3.2.2
and the following holds.

Claim 3.2.4
.

Let
\alpha
be a function in
(L^{\infty}\cap W^{2,2})
. Let
\beta
be a function in
\dot{W}^{-1,2}
Then the product
\alpha\beta
belongs to
\dot{W}^{-1,2}\oplus L^{4/3}\subset\dot{W}^{-1,2}
.

Proof.

Since
\beta\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}
, there exists
\gamma\in L^{2}
such that
D\gamma=\beta
. We have

\displaystyle\alpha\beta=D(\alpha\gamma)-\gamma D\alpha.

Now,
\alpha\in L^{\infty}
and
\gamma\in L^{2}
implies that
D(\alpha\gamma)\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}
. By the Sobolev embedding theorem,
\alpha\in W^{2,2}\subset W^{1,4}
so that
D\alpha\in L^{4}
and the product
\gamma D\alpha\in L^{4/3}
. By the dual of the Sobolev embedding theorem,
\alpha\beta\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}\oplus L^{4/3}\subset\dot{W}^{-1,2}.

∎

As a consequence, we have the norm

\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{W_{0}^{1,2}}\equiv\sup_{\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}}=1}\langle A,B\rangle_{0}\simeq\sup_{\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}}=1}\langle A,B\rangle.

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a ball. Suppose that
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. Let
u
be a weak solution of the problem

\begin{cases}\Delta u=f&\mbox{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\,\,\,\,\,u=0&\mbox{on}\,\,\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Then
D^{2}u\in L^{2}
and

||D^{2}u||_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim||f||_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Proof.

Clearly
\mathcal{L}[u]=|g|^{1/2}f
belongs to
L^{2}
since
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. A classical result of Miranda
[
54
]
(see also Corollary 1.4 in
[
20
]
) then yields the desired estimate

\left\lVert D^{2}u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert|g|^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

∎

Suppose that
u\in\Lambda^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
and
f=d^{\star}B
, where
B\in\Lambda^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, then (
3.2
) has the special structure

\Delta u=d^{\star}B

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[u_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}]\,\,dx^{i_{1}}\wedge_{4}\cdots\wedge_{4}dx^{i_{k}}

\displaystyle=|g|^{1/2}d^{\star}B

\displaystyle=\partial_{a}(|g|^{1/2}g^{ai_{k+1}}B_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k+1}})\,\,dx^{i_{1}}\wedge_{4}\cdots\wedge_{4}dx^{i_{k}}.

This is a decoupled system of
k
-uniformly elliptic equations in flat divergence form with coefficients in
W^{2,2}\cap L^{\infty}
which embeds in
VMO\cap L^{\infty}
. Such equations are studied by Di Fazio in
[
22
]
(see also
[
4
]
).

Proposition 3.2.6
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a ball. Suppose that
B\in\Lambda^{k+1}\otimes L^{p}(\Omega)
for
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Let
u\in\Lambda^{k}(\Omega)
be the
k
-form satisfying

\begin{cases}\Delta u=d^{\star}B&\mbox{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\,\,\,\,\,u=0&\mbox{on}\,\,\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

Then

\left\lVert Du\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

Proof.

Using Theorem 2.1 in
[
22
]
, each component of
u
satisfies

\left\lVert Du_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert|g|^{1/2}g^{ai_{k+1}}B_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k+1}}\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

∎

We will require the following corollaries of Theorem 2.1 in
[
22
]
.

Proposition 3.2.7
.

Let
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a ball. Suppose that
u\in\Lambda^{2}(\Omega)
satisfies the conditions

d^{\star}u=0\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad du\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega).

Then

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert du\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}.

Proof.

Let
\Psi\in\Lambda^{2}\otimes L^{2}(\Omega)
be arbitrary. Standard Hodge theory guarantees there exist
\psi\in\Lambda^{3}\otimes W^{1,2}(\Omega)
and
\theta\in\Lambda^{1}\otimes W^{1,2}(\Omega)
such that

\Psi=d\theta+d^{\star}\psi\quad\quad\mbox{with}\quad\quad d\psi=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad d^{\star}\theta=0.

We have, per Claim
3.2.4
,

\mathcal{L}[\psi]=|g|^{1/2}\Delta\psi=|g|^{1/2}d\Psi\,\,\in\,\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega).

Reasoning componentwise (set
\psi|_{\partial\Omega}=0
), we use
[
22
]
to arrive at

\displaystyle\left\lVert D\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert|g|^{1/2}d\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(3.3)

Observe that

\displaystyle\langle u,\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle u,d\theta+d^{\star}\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle du,\psi\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert du\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{cklh}}{\lesssim}\left\lVert du\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Hence the result

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert du\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}.

∎

Proposition 3.2.8
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a ball. Any
Q\in\Lambda^{3}(\Omega,\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))\otimes\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)
can be decomposed in the form

Q=dA+d^{\star}B\quad\quad\mbox{with}\quad\quad d^{\star}A=0\,,\,dB=0\,\,,\,\,A\in\Lambda^{2}\,\,,\,\,B\in\Lambda^{4}\,

and

\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}.

Proof.

Let
\Psi\in L^{2}(\Omega)
be arbitrary. As in the proof of Proposition
3.2.7
, we have

\Psi=d\theta+d^{\star}\psi\quad\mbox{with}\quad d^{\star}\theta=0\,,\,d\psi=0,

and

\displaystyle\left\lVert D\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert|g|^{1/2}d\Psi\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(3.4)

Since
\star B
is a 0-form and
d\psi=0
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\star B,\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\star B,d\theta+d^{\star}\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\star d^{\star}B,\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\star Q-d^{\star}(\star A),\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\star Q,\psi\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\star Q\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{neededd}}{\lesssim}\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

where Claim
3.2.4
has been used to obtain the last line. Hence

\displaystyle\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\simeq\left\lVert\star B\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}.

(3.5)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\langle A,\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle A,d\theta+d^{\star}\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle dA,\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle Q-d^{\star}B,\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle Q,\psi\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{W_{0}^{1,2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Hence

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}.

(3.6)

Combining the estimates (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) proves the result.
∎

We will state, without proof, the following Bourgain-Brezis type result whose non-trivial proof is amply outlined in Chapter 6 of
[
21
]
. E. Curca was kind enough to communicate with us the proof of Proposition
3.2.9
which was almost done in his thesis (see Theorem 6.4 in
[
21
]
and the discussion following Remark 6.5 on page 129 of
[
21
]
). The original predecessor of this result is due to Maz’ya
[
53
]
.

Proposition 3.2.9
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a ball and let
V\in\Lambda^{1}\otimes(L^{1}\oplus\dot{W}^{-2,2})
with
d^{\star}V=0.
There exists
w\in\Lambda^{2}\otimes\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)
such that

d^{\star}w=V\quad,\quad dw=0\quad,\quad\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{L^{1}\oplus\dot{W}^{-2,2}(\Omega)}.

Finally, we will need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 3.2.10
.

Let
\Omega
be a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. For
k\in(0,1)
, we denote by
\Omega_{k}
, the ball with same center as
\Omega
and with radius rescaled by
k
. Suppose
T\in\Lambda^{1}\otimes L^{2}(\Omega)
satisfies

\left\lVert dT\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert d^{\star}T\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}\lesssim M,

for some constant
M>0
. Then

\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{k})}\lesssim M+|\Omega|^{1/4}\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be a standard smooth cut-off function with

\mu|_{\Omega_{k}}=1\quad,\quad\mu|_{\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\Omega}=0\quad,\quad 0<\mu(x)<1\,\,\,\forall\,\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}.

Clearly,

\displaystyle\left\lVert d(\mu T)\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert d\mu\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\left\lVert dT\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\Omega|^{1/4}\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+M\,,

(3.7)

where we have used Jensen’s inequality. By the same token, using

d^{\star}(\mu T)=\star(d\mu\wedge_{4}\star T)+\mu d^{\star}T\,,

we find

\displaystyle\left\lVert d^{\star}(\mu T)\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}\lesssim|\Omega|^{1/4}\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+M\,.

(3.8)

Next, let
\Psi\in\Lambda^{1}\otimes L^{2}(\Omega)
be arbitrary. Consider the problem (componentwise)

\Delta\psi=\Psi\quad,\quad\psi|_{\partial\Omega}=0\quad,\quad\psi\in\Lambda^{1}\,.

Per Proposition
3.2.5
and the Sobolev embedding theorem, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert d\psi\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert d^{\star}\psi\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert D^{2}\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(3.9)

Next, we compute

\displaystyle\langle\mu T,\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mu T,\Delta\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mu T,dd^{\star}\psi+d^{\star}d\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d^{\star}(\mu T),d^{\star}\psi\rangle+\langle d(\mu T),d\psi\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{1b1},\eqref{1b2},\eqref{1b3}}{\lesssim}(|\Omega|^{1/4}\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+M)\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

This shows that

\left\lVert\mu T\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim|\Omega|^{1/4}\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+M\,.

Since
\mu\equiv 1
on
\Omega_{k},
the desired conclusion follows.

∎

The energy
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
cannot be assumed to be small since it is not bounded from below. Instead, we rescale our domain so as to obtain a ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
of arbitrary center and radius with

\vec{\left\lVert h\right\rVert}_{L^{4}(B)}<\varepsilon,

where
\varepsilon>0
may be chosen as small as we please.
A particular useful quantity is the 2-vector-valued 2-form

\vec{\eta}:=d\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}.

Proposition 3.2.11
.

It holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B)}<\varepsilon

(3.10)

Proof.

It suffices to realise that

d^{\star}\vec{\eta}=(4\vec{H}\wedge\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}+\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\vec{h}_{ij})\,\,dx^{j}

and
\vec{h}\in L^{4}(B)
,
d\vec{\Phi}\in L^{\infty}(B)
and
||\vec{H}||_{L^{4}(B)}\lesssim||\vec{h}||_{L^{4}(B)}\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

3.3
The creation of three characteristic two-forms

Let

E(\Omega):=\left\lVert DH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\Omega)}<\infty.

We have seen in Chapter
2
using translation invariance of
\mathcal{E}(\Sigma)
that there exists a divergence free tensor
\vec{V}
, that is
\nabla_{j}\vec{V}^{j}=\vec{0}
. Accordingly, we need to solve

d\star\vec{L}_{0}=\star\vec{V}

for
\vec{L}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{5})
and understand the regularity of
\vec{L}_{0}
. We will call upon Proposition
3.2.9
, but we need to first understand the regularity of
\vec{V}
. The exact expression for
\vec{V}
(see Theorem
2.4.2
) reveals that

\vec{V}^{j}=\nabla^{j}(\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H})+\vec{a}^{j}+\nabla_{k}(\vec{b}^{kj})+\vec{f}^{j}\,\,,

with

\displaystyle\left\lVert\vec{a}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\vec{\left\lVert b\right\rVert}_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}\lesssim E(\Omega)

(3.11)

and

\vec{f}^{j}\simeq(\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H})\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}.

We can write

\vec{f}=\nabla_{k}(h^{ij}\nabla^{k}H\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})-\vec{h}^{ij}h_{ik}\nabla^{k}H-(\nabla^{k}H\nabla_{k}h^{ij})\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi},

so that

\vec{\left\lVert f\right\rVert}_{L^{1}\oplus\dot{W}^{-1,4/3}(\Omega)}\lesssim E(\Omega).

Altogether, we find

\vec{\left\lVert V\right\rVert}_{\dot{W}^{-2,2}\oplus L^{1}(\Omega)}\lesssim E(\Omega),

where we have used that
\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}
and the fact that
\dot{W}^{-1,4/3}\subset\dot{W}^{-2,2}
by the dual of the Sobolev injection. Now, we call upon Proposition
3.2.9
to obtain
\vec{w}\in\Lambda^{2}\otimes\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)
with
d^{\star}\vec{w}=\vec{V}

and

\displaystyle\vec{\left\lVert w\right\rVert}_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\vec{\left\lVert V\right\rVert}_{\dot{W}^{-2,2}\oplus L^{1}(\Omega)}\lesssim E(\Omega).

(3.12)

We then set
\vec{L}_{0}:=\vec{w}
, so that
d\star\vec{L}_{0}=\star\vec{V}
. From (
3.12
) we have that
\vec{L}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}\otimes\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)
. Per Claim
3.2.4
, we know that the operator
\star
preserves
\dot{W}^{-1,2}
so that
\star\vec{L}_{0}\in\Lambda^{2}\otimes\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)
with the estimate

\displaystyle\vec{\left\lVert\star L_{0}\right\rVert}_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim E(\Omega).

(3.13)

Defining as in Proposition
2.6.3
the vector-valued 2-form

\star\vec{L}=\vec{L}_{0}-dH^{2}\wedge_{4}d\vec{\Phi},

we see that
\vec{L}\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}
with
d\vec{L}=d\star\vec{L}_{0}
and the estimate

\displaystyle\vec{\left\lVert L\right\rVert}_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\vec{\left\lVert\star L_{0}\right\rVert}_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\nabla H\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\overset{\eqref{tenn}}{\lesssim}E(\Omega),

(3.14)

where we have used that
L^{4/3}
injects continuously into
\dot{W}^{-1,2}
which is a consequence of the Sobolev injection
W_{0}^{1,2}\subset L^{4}
.
As in Proposition
2.6.3
, there exists 2-forms
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
satisfying

dS_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{.}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\quad\mbox{and}\quad d\vec{R}_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}+\star d\vec{u}-(\vec{J}+8H^{2}\vec{H})\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}

where
\vec{J}:=\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}+\frac{1}{2}|\vec{h}|^{2}\vec{H}-7|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}
.

In addition, we are free to demand
d^{\star}S_{0}=0
and
d^{\star}\vec{R}_{0}=\vec{0}
.

Next is to understand the regularity of
\vec{R}_{0}
and
S_{0}
. Recalling the definition of
\vec{J}
(see Proposition
2.6.3
) and the fact that
\Delta_{\perp}\vec{H}\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}
, we find that
\vec{J}\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}
. It remains to study the regularity of
d\vec{u}
.
To this end we have defined (see Proposition
2.6.3
)

\frac{5}{3}H^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}=d\vec{u}+d^{\star}\vec{v},\quad\quad\vec{u}\in\Lambda^{0}(\Omega,\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5})).

Clearly
d^{\star}\vec{u}=\vec{0}
. Let
\vec{\Theta}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
be arbitrary. Consider the problem

\Delta\vec{\theta}=\vec{\Theta}\quad\mbox{on}\,\,\Omega\quad\mbox{with}\,\,\,\vec{\theta}|_{\partial\Omega}=\vec{0}.

Proposition
3.2.5
confirms that

\displaystyle\vec{\left\lVert D\theta\right\rVert}_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\lesssim\vec{\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert}_{W_{0}^{2,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\vec{\left\lVert\Theta\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(3.15)

Observe that

\displaystyle\langle\vec{\Theta},\vec{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Delta\vec{\theta},\vec{u}\rangle=\langle d\vec{\theta},d\vec{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d\vec{\theta},H^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}-d^{\star}\vec{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d\vec{\theta},H^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert H^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}\vec{\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert}_{L^{4}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert H^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}\vec{\left\lVert\Theta\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

This shows that

\displaystyle\vec{\left\lVert u\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert H^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert H\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\left\lVert d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\overset{\eqref{ssssj}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon E(\Omega).

(3.16)

We have observed from equation (
2.52
) that
\vec{u}\in\Lambda^{0}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{5})
satisfies

\Delta\vec{u}=\frac{5}{3}d^{\star}(H^{2}d^{\star}\vec{\eta}),

which is of the type investigated in Proposition
3.2.6
.

We split
\vec{u}=\vec{u}_{0}+\vec{u}_{1}
where

\begin{cases}\Delta\vec{u}_{0}=\vec{0}&\mbox{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\,\,\,\,\,\vec{u}_{0}=\vec{u}&\mbox{on}\,\,\partial\Omega,\end{cases}\quad\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad\begin{cases}\Delta\vec{u}_{1}=\Delta\vec{u}&\mbox{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\,\,\,\,\,\vec{u}_{1}=\vec{0}&\mbox{on}\,\,\partial\Omega,\end{cases}

The equation for
\vec{u}_{1}
is handled as in Proposition
3.2.6
. We find

\displaystyle\left\lVert D\vec{u}_{1}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert H^{2}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\overset{\eqref{ssssj}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon E(\Omega).

(3.17)

In particular, the Sobolev embedding theorem gives

\displaystyle\left\lVert\vec{u}_{1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\varepsilon E(\Omega).

Hence by (
3.16
)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\vec{u}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\varepsilon E(\Omega).

(3.18)

On the other hand, the Caccioppoli inequality yields
1
1

1

The closure of
\Omega^{\prime}
is compact in
\Omega
.

\displaystyle\left\lVert D\vec{u}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega^{\prime})}\lesssim\left\lVert D\vec{u}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})}\lesssim\left\lVert\vec{u}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\overset{\eqref{pio}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon E(\Omega)\quad\quad\forall\,\,\,\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega

(3.19)

Together with (
3.17
), the latter gives

\displaystyle\left\lVert D\vec{u}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega^{\prime})}\lesssim\varepsilon E(\Omega)\quad\quad\forall\,\,\,\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega.

(3.20)

Using Claim (
3.2.4
) and the estimates we have of the quantities
\vec{u}
,
\vec{J}
and
\vec{L}
, we are now ready to state the first regularity property:

dS_{0}\,\,,\,\,d\vec{R}_{0}\in\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)

with the estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert dS_{0}\right\rVert_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}+||d\vec{R}_{0}||_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim E(\Omega_{1})\quad\quad\forall\,\,\Omega\Subset\Omega_{1}.

(3.21)

Since
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
are co-closed, by Proposition (
3.2.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+||\vec{R}_{0}||_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim E(\Omega_{1})\quad\quad\Omega\Subset\Omega_{1}.

(3.22)

Remark 3.3.1
.

Note that

\displaystyle\left\lVert d^{\star}\vec{v}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega^{\prime})}\lesssim\left\lVert d\vec{u}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(\Omega^{\prime})}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\left\lVert d^{\star}\vec{\eta}\right\rVert_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\overset{\eqref{ssssj},\eqref{host}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon E(\Omega_{1}).

(3.23)

Our next goal is to obtain an estimate similar to (
3.21
) but with
L^{4/3}
norm in place of
\dot{W}^{-1,2}
. In order to do this, we first introduce an operator
\vec{\mathcal{P}}
which helps to redefine
\vec{L}
.
Next, we redefine the two-forms
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
in order to create
S
and
\vec{R}
respectively. It turns out that
dS
and
d\vec{R}
can then be estimated in
L^{4/3}
as desired (this is done in Section
3.4
). The remaining part of the present section is devoted to introducing
\vec{\mathcal{P}}
and redefining
\vec{L}
,
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

The operator

\mathcal{\vec{P}}:\Lambda^{1}(\Sigma,\Lambda^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}(\Sigma,\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))\quad\quad\mbox{defined by}\quad\quad\mathcal{\vec{P}}:\vec{\ell}\longmapsto\vec{\ell}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}

is algebraically invertible. Moreover
\mathcal{\vec{P}}^{-1}
injects
L^{2}
into itself continuously.

Proof.

We have

\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}:=\big(\mathcal{\vec{P}}(\vec{\ell})\big)_{pq}=\vec{\ell}_{p}\wedge\nabla_{q}\vec{\Phi}-\vec{\ell}_{q}\wedge\nabla_{p}\vec{\Phi}.

Let
\vec{n}
be the normal vector to the Willmore-type hypersurface
\Sigma
, defined on a small patch
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}

\vec{n}:=\star_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}\star(d\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})\in\Lambda^{0}(\Lambda^{1})

where
\star_{\mathbb{R}^{5}}
acts on the 4-vectors in the ambient space while
\star
acts on the 4-forms in the parameter space.

We compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}\cdot(\vec{n}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=(\vec{\ell}_{p}\wedge\nabla_{q}\vec{\Phi}-\vec{\ell}_{q}\wedge\nabla_{p}\vec{\Phi})\cdot(\vec{n}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=4\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\ell}_{p}-\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\ell}_{p}

\displaystyle=3\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\ell}_{p}\,,

so that

\displaystyle\vec{\eta}\cdot\vec{\ell}_{p}=\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}\cdot(\vec{n}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}).

(3.24)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}\cdot(\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=(\vec{\ell}_{p}\wedge\nabla_{q}\vec{\Phi}-\vec{\ell}_{q}\wedge\nabla_{p}\vec{\Phi})\cdot(\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=2(4\vec{\ell}_{p}\cdot\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}-\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{\ell}_{p})

\displaystyle=6\vec{\ell}_{p}\cdot\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\,,

so that

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}_{p}\cdot\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}=\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}\cdot(\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}).

(3.25)

Finally, we check that

\displaystyle\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}\cdot(\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=(\vec{\ell}_{p}\wedge\nabla_{q}\vec{\Phi}-\vec{\ell}_{q}\wedge\nabla_{p}\vec{\Phi})\cdot(\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=2\vec{\ell}_{p}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}+\delta^{i}_{p}\vec{\ell}_{q}\cdot\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{sothat}}{=}2\vec{\ell}_{p}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}+\frac{1}{6}\delta^{i}_{p}\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{sq}\cdot(\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}),

so that

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}_{p}\cdot\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}\cdot(\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})-\frac{1}{12}\delta^{i}_{p}\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{sq}\cdot(\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}).

(3.26)

According to (
3.24
) and (
3.26
), the 1-form
\vec{\ell}
can be totally recovered from
\mathcal{\vec{P}}
via

\vec{\ell}_{p}=\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}\cdot(\nabla^{i}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})-\frac{1}{12}\delta^{i}_{p}\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{sq}\cdot(\nabla^{s}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})\right]\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}+\left[\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{\vec{P}}_{pq}\cdot(\vec{n}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})\right]\vec{n}.

That
\mathcal{\vec{P}}^{-1}
maps
L^{2}
onto itself continuously is trivial.

∎

Observe that given any
\vec{\mu}\in\Lambda^{2}(\Omega,\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))\otimes L^{2}(\Omega)
, we can define

\displaystyle\vec{\ell}:=\mathcal{\vec{P}}^{-1}(\vec{\mu}).

(3.27)

We now create a new 2-form

\vec{L}_{1}:=\vec{L}+d\vec{\ell}

satisfying

\displaystyle\vec{\left\lVert L_{1}\right\rVert}_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\vec{\left\lVert L\right\rVert}_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}+\vec{\left\lVert\ell\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\overset{\eqref{ax}}{\lesssim}E(\Omega_{1})+||\vec{\mu}||_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Consider now

\displaystyle S:=S_{0}+\vec{\ell}\overset{.}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad\vec{R}:=\vec{R}_{0}+\vec{\ell}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}.

(3.28)

We immediately see that

dS=\vec{L}_{1}\overset{.}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\quad\mbox{and}\quad d\vec{R}=\vec{L}_{1}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}+\star d\vec{u}-(\vec{J}+8H^{2}\vec{H})\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}.

3.4
Estimating
S
and
\vec{R}
in the correct space

From Chapter
2
, Proposition
2.6.3
, the two-forms
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
satisfy

dS_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad d\vec{R}_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}+\star d\vec{u}-\vec{J}_{0}\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}

where for convenience, we have set
\vec{J}_{0}:=\vec{J}+8H^{2}\vec{H}.

We compute

\displaystyle\quad\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}\star(d\vec{R}_{0}-\star d\vec{u}+\vec{J}_{0}\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk[r}\epsilon_{ab]pq}\vec{L}^{ij}\cdot\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\,g^{kq}\,\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=\vec{L}_{[ab}\cdot\nabla_{r]}\vec{\Phi}\,\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=dS_{0}.

As
\vec{J}_{0}
is a normal vector, we have

\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}(\vec{J}_{0}\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi})=0.

Hence

\displaystyle dS_{0}=\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}\star d\vec{R}_{0}-\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{u}.

(3.29)

By a similar token, we find (see Appendix
B
for the full computation)

\displaystyle\quad\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}\star(d\vec{R}_{0}-\star d\vec{u}+\vec{J}_{0}\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk[r}\epsilon_{ab]pq}(\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi})\bullet(\vec{L}^{ij}\wedge\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi})\,\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ijk[r}\epsilon_{ab]pq}\left[(\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{L}^{ij})\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}+g^{qk}\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\vec{L}^{ij}\right]

\displaystyle=\star\vec{\eta}\wedge_{4}\star dS_{0}-(d\vec{R}_{0}-\star d\vec{u}+\vec{J}_{0}\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi})+\vec{Q}_{0}

(3.30)

where
\vec{Q}_{0}\in\Lambda^{3}(\Omega,\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5}))
is given by

\vec{Q}_{0}:=\frac{1}{12}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\left[(\vec{L}_{k\alpha}\cdot\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi})\nabla_{\beta}\vec{\Phi}-(\vec{L}_{k\alpha}\cdot\nabla_{\beta}\vec{\Phi})\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}\right]\wedge\nabla_{\gamma}\vec{\Phi}\,\,\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}.

As done in Appendix
B
, we find

\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}(\vec{J}_{0}\wedge d\vec{\Phi})=-\vec{J}_{0}\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}.

Hence from equation (
3.30
), we have

\displaystyle d\vec{R}_{0}=\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}\star d\vec{R}_{0}+\star\vec{\eta}\wedge_{4}\star dS_{0}-\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{u}-\star d\vec{u}+\vec{Q}_{0}.

(3.31)

We continue with one trivial identity which holds by the antisymmetry of
(\star\vec{R}_{0})
and
(\star S_{0})
,

\displaystyle\quad\quad(\nabla_{l}\vec{\eta}_{ij}+\nabla_{j}\vec{\eta}_{il})\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}+(\nabla_{l}\vec{\eta}_{ij}+\nabla_{j}\vec{\eta}_{il})(\star S_{0})^{lj}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{l}\vec{\eta}_{ij}-\nabla_{l}\vec{\eta}_{ij})\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}+(\nabla_{l}\vec{\eta}_{ij}-\nabla_{l}\vec{\eta}_{ij})(\star S_{0})^{lj}

\displaystyle=\vec{0}.

As
d\vec{\eta}=\vec{0}
, that is,
\nabla_{i}\vec{\eta}_{jl}+\nabla_{j}\vec{\eta}_{li}+\nabla_{l}\vec{\eta}_{ij}=\vec{0}
, the latter yields

\displaystyle(-\nabla_{i}\vec{\eta}_{jl}+2\nabla_{j}\vec{\eta}_{il})\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}+(-\nabla_{i}\vec{\eta}_{jl}+2\nabla_{j}\vec{\eta}_{il})(\star S_{0})^{lj}=\vec{0}.

(3.32)

We next compute

\displaystyle\quad\nabla^{j}(\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}+\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star S_{0})^{il})

\displaystyle=\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet\nabla^{j}(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}+(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}+\vec{\eta}_{jl}\nabla^{j}(\star{S}_{0})^{il}+(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}(\star{S}_{0})^{il}

\displaystyle=-\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet\big(\nabla^{l}(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{ji}+\nabla^{i}(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}\big)+(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\vec{\eta}_{jl}\big(\nabla^{l}(\star{S}_{0})^{ji}+\nabla^{i}(\star{S}_{0})^{lj}\big)+(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}(\star{S}_{0})^{il}

(3.33)

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{l}\big(\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{ji}\big)+\nabla^{l}\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{ji}+(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\nabla^{i}\big(\vec{\eta}_{lj}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}\big)-\nabla^{i}\vec{\eta}_{lj}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\nabla^{l}\big(\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star{S}_{0})^{ji}\big)+\nabla^{l}\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star S_{0})^{ji}+(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}(\star S_{0})^{il}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\nabla^{i}\big(\vec{\eta}_{lj}(\star{S}_{0})^{lj}\big)-\nabla^{i}\vec{\eta}_{lj}(\star{S}_{0})^{lj}

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{j}\big(\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}+\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star S_{0})^{il}\big)+\nabla^{i}\big(\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{jl}+\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star S_{0})^{jl}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\nabla^{i}\vec{\eta}_{lj}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}-\nabla^{i}\vec{\eta}_{lj}(\star S_{0})^{lj}+2(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}+2(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}(\star{S}_{0})^{il}

where we have used that
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
are co-closed to obtain (
3.33
).

Hence

\displaystyle\nabla^{j}\big(\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}+\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star S_{0})^{il}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{i}\big(\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{jl}+\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star S_{0})^{jl}\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla^{i}\vec{\eta}_{lj}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}+\nabla^{i}\vec{\eta}_{lj}(\star S_{0})^{lj}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}+(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}(\star{S}_{0})^{il}.

(3.34)

This yields

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left(\star(\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}\star d\vec{R}_{0}+\star\vec{\eta}\wedge_{4}\star dS_{0})\right)^{i}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{jkli}\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{jkpq}\big(\vec{\eta}^{pq}\bullet\nabla^{r}(\star\vec{R}_{0})_{rl}+\vec{\eta}^{pq}\nabla^{r}(\star S_{0})_{rl}\big)

\displaystyle=2\vec{\eta}^{li}\bullet\nabla^{j}(\star\vec{R}_{0})_{jl}+2\vec{\eta}^{li}\nabla^{j}(\star S_{0})_{jl}

\displaystyle=2\nabla^{j}\left(\vec{\eta}^{li}\bullet(\star{R}_{0})_{jl}+\vec{\eta}^{li}(\star{S}_{0})_{jl}\right)-2\nabla^{j}\vec{\eta}^{li}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})_{jl}-2\nabla^{j}\vec{\eta}^{li}(\star S_{0})_{jl}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{tyq}}{=}2\nabla^{j}\left(\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}-\vec{\eta}^{il}\bullet(\star{R}_{0})_{jl}+\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star{S}_{0})^{il}-\vec{\eta}^{il}(\star{S}_{0})_{jl}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\nabla^{i}\left(\vec{\eta}_{jl}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{jl}+\vec{\eta}_{jl}(\star{S}_{0})^{jl}\right)+\left(\nabla^{i}\vec{\eta}_{lj}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{lj}+\nabla^{i}\vec{\eta}_{lj}(\star{S}_{0})^{lj}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-2\nabla^{j}\vec{\eta}^{li}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})_{jl}-2\nabla^{j}\vec{\eta}^{li}(\star{S}_{0})_{jl}-2(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}-2(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}(\star{S}_{0})^{il}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{lassz}}{=}\nabla^{j}\mathchoice{\vec{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}+\nabla^{i}\vec{G}-4\nabla^{j}\vec{\eta}^{li}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})_{jl}-4\nabla^{j}\vec{\eta}^{li}(\star{S}_{0})_{jl}-2(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{il}-2(d^{\star}\vec{\eta})_{l}(\star{S}_{0})^{il}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{j}\mathchoice{\vec{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{P}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}+\nabla^{i}\vec{G}+\star\vec{Q}_{1},

where

\displaystyle\vec{P}_{ij}:=\mathchoice{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\bullet\mathchoice{(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{l}}}}{(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{l}}}}{(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{l}}}}{(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}{l}}}}-\mathchoice{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\bullet(\star{R}_{0})_{jl}+\mathchoice{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(\star{S}_{0})_{il}-\mathchoice{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{\eta}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{l}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.56946pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.11319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(\star{S}_{0})_{jl}

(3.35)

is the component of the two-vector valued 2-form
\vec{P}:=\vec{P}_{ij}\,\,dx^{i}\wedge_{4}dx^{j}
and

\vec{G}:=\vec{\eta}_{lj}\bullet(\star\vec{R}_{0})^{jl}+\vec{\eta}_{lj}(\star{S}_{0})^{jl}\,\,\,\in\Lambda^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5})).

Introducing (
3.31
), we find

\displaystyle\star d\vec{R}_{0}=d^{\star}\vec{P}+d\vec{G}+\vec{U}+\star\vec{Q},

(3.36)

where for notational convenience we have set

\displaystyle\vec{U}:=\star(\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{u}+\star d\vec{u}),\quad\quad\vec{Q}:=\vec{Q}_{0}+\vec{Q}_{1}.

(3.37)

By estimates (
3.14
), (
3.22
) and Claim (
3.2.4
), we have

\displaystyle||\vec{Q}||_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}\lesssim||\vec{L}||_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(\Omega)}+||\vec{R}_{0}||_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+||S_{0}||_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\overset{\eqref{ax},\eqref{ax1}}{\lesssim}E(\Omega_{1}).

(3.38)

Standard Hodge theory gives the decomposition

\vec{Q}=d\vec{A}+d^{\star}\vec{B}

\mbox{with}\quad d^{\star}\vec{A}=\vec{0}\,,\,d\vec{B}=\vec{0},\quad\quad\quad\vec{A}\in\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5})),\vec{B}\in\Lambda^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{4},\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{5})).

We will now write
\vec{R}
in terms of
\vec{G}
,
\star{B}
and
\vec{U}
and according to the definition (
3.27
) of
\vec{\ell}
.
Owing to Lemma
3.3.2
, we define

\vec{\ell}:=\mathcal{P}^{-1}(-\vec{A}-\star\vec{P}).

By Proposition
3.2.8
, we know that
\vec{A}
belongs to
L^{2}
; by estimate (
3.22
) and equation (
3.36
), we know that
\star\vec{P}
also belongs to
L^{2}
. Thus the sum
\vec{A}+\star\vec{P}
belongs to
L^{2}
and now
\vec{\ell}
is a suitable candidate to redefine
\vec{L}
.

Accordingly, from (
3.36
) we have

\displaystyle d(\vec{G}+\star\vec{B})+\vec{U}=\star d(\vec{R}_{0}-\vec{A}-\star\vec{P})=\star d(\vec{R}_{0}+\vec{\ell}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})\overset{\eqref{merio}}{=}\star d\vec{R}.

(3.39)

We finish this section with estimates. Let
\Omega_{1}
appearing in (
3.22
) be a ball
B_{r}
of fixed radius
r
. Let
\vec{F}:=\vec{G}+\star\vec{B}
, then we have

Lemma 3.4.1
.

It holds

\displaystyle||d\vec{F}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\lesssim(k+\varepsilon)E(B_{r})\quad\quad\forall\,\,0<k<1/2.

Proof.

Observe first that

\Delta\vec{F}=-d^{\star}\vec{U}.

To study this equation, note that

\displaystyle||\vec{G}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}\quad

\displaystyle\lesssim\quad||\vec{\eta}||_{L^{\infty}(B_{r/2})}\left(||\vec{R}_{0}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}+||S_{0}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ax1}}{\lesssim}E(B_{r}).

(3.40)

By Proposition (
3.2.8
), we have

\displaystyle||\star\vec{B}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}\lesssim||\vec{Q}||_{\dot{W}^{-1,2}(B_{r/2})}\overset{\eqref{lives}}{\lesssim}E(B_{r}).

(3.41)

Combining the estimates (
3.40
) and (
3.41
) yield

\displaystyle||\vec{F}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}\lesssim E(B_{r}).

(3.42)

We split
\vec{F}=\vec{F}_{0}+\vec{F}_{1}
such that

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}[\vec{F}_{0}]=\vec{0}&\mbox{in}\,\,B_{r/2}\\
\,\,\,\,\,\vec{F}_{0}=\vec{F}&\mbox{on}\,\,\partial B_{r/2},\end{cases}\quad\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}[\vec{F}_{1}]=|g|^{1/2}d^{\star}\vec{U}&\mbox{in}\,\,B_{r/2}\\
\,\,\,\,\,\vec{F}_{1}=\vec{0}&\mbox{on}\,\,\partial B_{r/2}.\end{cases}

Elliptic estimates (see Proposition
3.2.6
and recall
\vec{F}_{1}
is a 0-form) yield

\displaystyle||\vec{F}_{1}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}+||d\vec{F}_{1}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{r/2})}\lesssim||\vec{U}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{r/2})}.

(3.43)

On the other hand the Jensen and Caccioppoli inequalities give

\displaystyle||d\vec{F}_{0}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr/2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim kr||d\vec{F}_{0}||_{L^{2}(B_{kr/2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim kr||d\vec{F}_{0}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim k||\vec{F}_{0}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim k||\vec{F}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}+k||\vec{F}_{1}||_{L^{2}(B_{r/2})}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{amer}\eqref{amer1}}{\lesssim}||\vec{U}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{r/2})}+kE(B_{r}).

Combining the latter with (
3.43
) yields the estimate

\displaystyle||d\vec{F}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr/2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim||d\vec{F}_{0}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr/2})}+||d\vec{F}_{1}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{r/2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim||\vec{U}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{r/2})}+kE(B_{r}).

(3.44)

It remains to observe from (
3.37
) that

\displaystyle||\vec{U}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{r/2})}\lesssim||d\vec{u}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{r/2})}\overset{\eqref{host}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon E(B_{r}).

(3.45)

Relabelling the domains and choosing
0<k<1/2
rather than
0<k<1
, we obtain

||d\vec{F}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\lesssim(k+\varepsilon)E(B_{r}).

This completes the proof.

∎

Corollary 3.4.2
.

We have

\left\lVert dS\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}+||d\vec{R}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\lesssim(k+\varepsilon)E(B_{r})\quad\quad\forall\,\,k\in(0,1/2).

Proof.

It follows immediately from Lemma
3.4.1
, equation (
3.39
) and the estimate

\displaystyle||\vec{U}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\lesssim||d\vec{u}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\overset{\eqref{host}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon E(B_{r}).

(3.46)

that

\displaystyle||d\vec{R}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\lesssim(k+\varepsilon)E(B_{r}).

(3.47)

Recall that
dS
and
d\vec{R}_{0}
are defined from the modified
\vec{L}+d\vec{\ell}
. Thus
S
and
\vec{R}
form an admissible pair and are linked together by the same relation (
3.29
)
linking
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
, namely

dS=\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}\star d\vec{R}-\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{u}.

Calling upon (
3.47
) and (
3.20
) gives the desired estimate

\left\lVert dS\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\lesssim(k+\varepsilon)E(B_{r}).

∎

The advantage of
S
and
\vec{R}
over
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
is that Corollary
3.4.2
involves
L^{4/3}
norm rather than
\dot{W}^{-1,2}
norm in the estimate (
3.21
).

3.5
The return equation: controlling the geometry

We recall the return equation from Chapter
2
(precisely (
2.51
))
