12d^{\star}\vec{T}=\star(d\vec{R}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})+\star(dS\wedge_{4}d\vec{\Phi})+6\star(\star d^{\star}\vec{v}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})

where

\displaystyle\vec{T}:=(\nabla_{j}\vec{H}+2Hh_{jk}\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}-2H^{2}\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi})dx^{j}

(3.48)

and
\vec{v}
is as in Remark
3.3.1
. Note first that

\displaystyle\vec{\left\lVert T\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}\lesssim\left\lVert dH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{kr})}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{kr)}}\lesssim E(B_{kr})\lesssim E(B_{r}).

(3.49)

On the other hand, we find from (
3.48
)

\displaystyle d^{\star}\vec{T}=\nabla^{j}\vec{T}_{j}=\Delta\vec{H}+2\nabla^{j}(Hh_{jk}\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}-H^{2}\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi}).

(3.50)

Using (
3.50
) and introducing Remark
3.3.1
and Corollary
3.4.2
into the return equation yields for all
k\in(0,1/2)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta H\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\lesssim||d^{\star}\vec{T}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}+\varepsilon E(B_{r})\lesssim(k+\varepsilon)E(B_{r}).

(3.51)

On the other hand, a direct computation reveals

(d\vec{T})_{ij}=2(h_{ik}\nabla_{j}H-h_{jk}\nabla_{i}H)\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}+2\nabla_{j}H^{2}\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}-2\nabla_{i}H^{2}\nabla_{j}\vec{\Phi},

so that

\displaystyle||d\vec{T}||_{L^{4/3}(B_{kr})}\lesssim\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{kr})}\left\lVert dH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}\lesssim\varepsilon E(B_{r}).

(3.52)

Using (
3.49
), (
3.51
) and (
3.52
) into Lemma
3.2.10
yields

\displaystyle||\vec{T}||_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}\lesssim(\varepsilon+k)E(B_{r}).

(3.53)

We now find

\displaystyle\left\lVert dH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{nameT}}{\lesssim}||\vec{T}||_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{kr})}\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{kr})}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{hg1}}{\lesssim}(\varepsilon+k)E(B_{r})+\varepsilon E(B_{r})

\displaystyle\lesssim(\varepsilon+k)E(B_{r}).

(3.54)

By the Sobolev embedding theorem and the Jensen’s inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{kr})}\lesssim\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}+\left\lVert dH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}\overset{\eqref{ja1b}}{\lesssim}(\varepsilon+k)E(B_{r}).

(3.55)

Combining (
3.54
) and (
3.55
), we have the crucial estimate

E(B_{kr})\equiv\left\lVert dH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{kr})}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{kr})}\lesssim(\varepsilon+k)E(B_{r}).

Since
\varepsilon
and
k
may be chosen as small as we please, by a standard controlled growth argument (cf. Lemma 5.13 in
[
28
]
), we find the Morrey decay

\displaystyle E(B_{r})\equiv\left\lVert DH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{r})}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{r})}\lesssim E(B_{1})r^{\beta}\quad\quad\forall\,\,r<1.

(3.56)

We emphasise that this is true for any
\beta\in(0,1)
.

Consider the maximal function

\mathcal{M}_{3-\beta}[f]:=\sup_{\rho>0}\rho^{-1-\beta}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(B_{\rho})}.

By Jensen’s inequality, we have

\mathcal{M}_{3-\beta}[f]\lesssim\sup_{\rho>0}\rho^{-\beta}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(B_{\rho})}.

Using the Morrey decay (
3.56
) and (
3.49
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{3-\beta}[\Delta\vec{H}]\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}\lesssim E(B_{1})\quad\quad\forall\,\,r<1.

(3.57)

Let
f
be a locally integrable function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Recall that for a number
\alpha
satisfying
\alpha\in(0,n)
, the
Riesz potential
\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}
of order
\alpha

of
f
is defined by the convolution

(\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}*f)(x):=\frac{1}{C(\alpha)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(y)|x-y|^{\alpha-n}dy

where
C(\alpha)=\pi^{n/2}2^{\alpha}\,\,\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})/\Gamma(\frac{n-\alpha}{2})
is a constant.

We will now use the following result from
[
1
]
.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

If
\alpha>0
,
0<\lambda\leq n
,
1<p<\lambda/\alpha
,
1\leq q\leq\infty
, and
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda/p}[f]\in L^{q}(\Omega)
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then

\left\lVert\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}[f]\right\rVert_{L^{r}(\Omega)}\lesssim\left\lVert M_{\lambda/p}[f]\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Omega)}^{\alpha p/\lambda}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{p}^{1-\alpha p/\lambda}

where
1/r=1/p-\alpha/\lambda+(\alpha p)/(\lambda q)
.

Putting
\alpha=1
,
q=\infty
,
p=4/3
,
\lambda=4(3-\beta)/3
in Proposition
3.5.1
, we find

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{I}_{1}[\Delta\vec{H}]\right\rVert_{L^{s}(B_{r})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{3-\beta}[\Delta\vec{H}]\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}^{1/(3-\beta)}\left\lVert\Delta\vec{H}\right\rVert_{L^{4/3}(B_{r})}^{(2-\beta)/(3-\beta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim E(B_{1})^{1-\frac{4}{3s}}E(B_{r})^{\frac{4}{3s}}

(3.58)

where

s:=\frac{4}{3}\left(\frac{3-\beta}{2-\beta}\right)\in\left(2,\frac{8}{3}\right)

and
\mathcal{I}_{1}
denotes the Riesz potential of order 1.

Let now
s\in\left(2,\frac{8}{3}\right)
. Using standard elliptic estimates and the Sobolev embedding theorem, we have for all
r<1

\displaystyle\left\lVert D\vec{H}\right\rVert_{L^{s}(B_{r})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\mathcal{I}_{1}[\Delta\vec{H}]\right\rVert_{L^{s}(B_{r})}+r^{2-\frac{4}{s}}\left\lVert D\vec{H}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{1})}

\displaystyle\lesssim E(B_{1})^{1-\frac{4}{3s}}E(B_{r})^{\frac{4}{3s}}+r^{2-\frac{4}{s}}E(B_{1}).

(3.59)

As (
3.59
) holds for all
s\in\left(2,\frac{8}{3}\right)
, we see that the integrability of
D\vec{H}
has been improved.

With this new information on the integrability of
d\vec{H}
, the above procedure may be repeated untill we obtain that
\vec{H}
is Lipschitz. Once this is known, we see that
\Delta\vec{\Phi}
is as well Lipschitz. Since
|g|^{1/2}\in W^{1,4}
by hypothesis, we see that
|g|^{1/2}\Delta\vec{\Phi}
lies in
W^{1,4}
. We have

\mathcal{L}[\vec{\Phi}]=|g|^{1/2}\Delta\vec{\Phi}.

Calling upon Theorem 1.1 in
[
36
]
, the Green kernel
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{L}
satisfies
D\mathcal{G}\in L^{{4/3},\infty}
where
L^{{4/3},\infty}
is the
2
2

2

The weak-
L^{4/3}
Marcinkiewicz space
L^{{4/3},\infty}(B_{1}(0))
is defined as those functions
f
which satisfy
\sup_{\alpha>0}\alpha^{4/3}|\{x\in B_{1}(0):|f(x)|\geq\alpha\}|<\infty
. The space
L^{{4/3},\infty}
is also a Lorentz space.
weak Marcinkiewicz space.

Formally, the solution
\vec{\Phi}

is given by the convolution

\vec{\Phi}=\mathcal{G}*\mathcal{L}[\vec{\Phi}].

Hence by the convolution rule for Lorentz spaces (cf.
[
42
]
)

D^{2}\vec{\Phi}=D\mathcal{G}*D\mathcal{L}[\vec{\Phi}]\in L^{{4/3},\infty}*L^{4}\subset L^{p}\quad\quad\forall\,\,p<\infty.

Accordingly, by the Sobolev embedding theorem,
D\vec{\Phi}\in\bigcap_{p<\infty}W^{1,p}\subset\bigcap_{\alpha<1}C^{0,\alpha}
. The regularity of
\vec{\Phi}
has thus also improved. In particular, the metric coefficients are Hölder continuous of all orders, and it follows that the standard analysis of second-order uniformly elliptic operators is now at hand (cf.
[
29
]
). Eventually, by standard elliptic arguments, we reach the conclusion that
\vec{\Phi}
is smooth.

Finally, by standard elliptic arguments we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\lVert DH\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}+\frac{1}{r}\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r})}\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\left\lVert DH\right\rVert_{L^{2}(B_{1})}+\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{L^{4}(B_{1})}\right)\quad\quad\forall\,r<1.

(3.60)

This concludes the proof.

Chapter 4
Final Thoughts and Openings

In the previous Chapters, we presented a conformally invariant four dimensional generalisation of the Willmore energy and proved, via conservation laws, that critical points of this energy are regular. There are other important analysis aspects of this energy that are yet to be explored. Our goal here is not to outline four dimensional analogues of every result in literature concerning two dimensional Willmore surfaces, as there are some results that cannot be formulated for the energy (
1.5
). For instance, we do not have Willmore-type conjectures
1
1

1

The Willmore conjecture
[
68
]
simply says that in the class of immersed tori, the Clifford torus has the minimal Willmore energy of
2\pi^{2}
.
for the energy (
1.5
) as the energy is not bounded below (see Proposition 1.1 in
[
35
]
and the discussion beneath it). In the present Chapter, we outline some interesting analysis aspects of the energy (
1.5
) that are directly related to our work.

4.1
Robert Bryant’s formalism

In his seminal article
[
13
]
, Robert Bryant introduced the following formalism for studying the Willmore immersions of a surface via its conformal Gauss map. Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an immersion with unit normal vector
\vec{n}
. Let
\mathbb{S}^{4,1}
be the deSitter space. The conformal Gauss map
\vec{Y}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{4,1}
is defined via

\displaystyle\vec{Y}:=H\vec{X}+\vec{N}

where

\displaystyle\vec{X}:=\begin{pmatrix}\vec{\Phi}\\
\frac{1}{2}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}-1)\\
\frac{1}{2}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}+1)\end{pmatrix}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad\vec{N}:=\begin{pmatrix}\vec{n}\\
\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\Phi}\\
\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}

One can easily show
2
2

2

See Appendix
C
that

\displaystyle d\vec{Y}=\vec{X}dH-(h_{0})d\vec{X}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad\langle\nabla_{i}\vec{Y},\nabla_{j}\vec{Y}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{4,1}}=(h_{0})_{ik}(h_{0})^{k}_{j}=\frac{1}{2}|h_{0}|^{2}g_{ij}

where
\mathbb{R}^{4,1}
is the Minkowski space and
\mathbb{S}^{4,1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4,1}
. Thus the Dirichlet energy of
\vec{Y}
in the deSitter space is the same as the Willmore energy of
\vec{\Phi}
, that is

\|d\vec{Y}\rVert_{\mathbb{S}^{4,1}}^{2}=\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{2}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

One can see that the immersion
\vec{\Phi}
is Willmore if and only if
\vec{Y}
is minimal (see
[
24
]
).
In addition, by defining the quartic form

Q:=\langle\vec{Y}_{zz},\vec{Y}_{zz}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}^{4,1}}\,\,dz^{4},

where
z\in\mathbb{C}
is a local coordinate, Bryant was able to prove that
\vec{\Phi}
is Willmore if and only if
Q
is holomorphic. This leads us to ask if the Bryant formalism can be reproduced in four dimensions for the energy (
1.5
).
Preliminary computations
3
3

3

See Appendix
C
show that

\lVert\Delta\vec{Y}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{6,1}}=\int_{\Sigma}-2|\nabla H|^{2}+|h_{0}|^{4}\,\,d\textnormal{vol}_{g}.

Although the quantity
\lVert\Delta\vec{Y}\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{S}^{6,1}}
expressed above significantly differs from the energy (
1.5
) (in codimension one), it reproduces the leading term
|\nabla H|^{2}
and a conformally invariant term
|h_{0}|^{4}
. This observation leads us to believe that there is a link between
\vec{Y}
and
\vec{\Phi}
.

4.2
Frédéric Hélein’s Coulomb frames

The well-known uniformisation theorem classifies Riemann surfaces into three different types. It says that every Riemann surface is conformally equivalent to either the unit disk, the complex plane, or the Riemann sphere.
The induced metric
g
on any
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfies
g=e^{2\lambda}\delta
for some conformal parameter
\lambda
. There is a link between the Willmore energy of
\Sigma
and the regularity of
\lambda
. Since the parameter
\lambda
can be viewed as a “distortion” ratio between a flat disk and its image on
\Sigma
, the geometry of the surface
\Sigma
can be understood via its Willmore energy. In Chapter 5 of
[
39
]
, Hélein presented an approach to the study of Willmore surfaces via the introduction of
Coulomb frames.
4
4

4

See also
[
62
]

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an immersion and let
\Gamma(\mathcal{T}\vec{\Phi}(\Sigma))
be a section of the tangent bundle to
\vec{\Phi}(\Sigma)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. A
moving frame
on
\vec{\Phi}
is a pair
\vec{e}:=(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2})\in\Gamma(\mathcal{T}\vec{\Phi}(\Sigma))\times\Gamma(\mathcal{T}\vec{\Phi}(\Sigma))
if for every point
x:=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\Sigma
, the pair
\vec{e}\,(x_{1},x_{2})
is a positive
5
5

5

That is, the frame
\vec{e}
agrees with a fixed orientation of
\vec{\Phi}(\Sigma)
.
orthormal basis of
\mathcal{T}_{x}\vec{\Phi}(\Sigma)
. A
Coulomb frame
is a frame
\vec{e}
that satisfies the following
Coulomb condition
:

d^{\star}(\vec{e}_{1}\cdot d\vec{e}_{2})=0.

Owing to the Gauss equation and the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, the Willmore energy can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}|h|^{2}+\pi\chi(\Sigma)d\textnormal{vol}_{g}

(4.1)

where
\chi(\Sigma)
is a topological invariant.
By controlling the Willmore energy of
\Sigma
, Hélein (Lemma 5.1.4 of
[
39
]
) was able to construct a Coulomb frame with good regularity properies:

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Suppose that the Willmore energy satisfies

\int_{D_{1}(0)}|h|^{2}d\textnormal{vol}_{g}<\frac{8\pi}{3}

then there exists a frame
\vec{e}\in W^{1,2}(D_{1}(0))
such that

\lVert d\vec{e}_{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(D_{1/2}(0))}+\lVert d\vec{e}_{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(D_{1/2}(0))}\lesssim\lVert h\rVert_{L^{2}(D_{1}(0))}.

A simple computation gives a link
relating the conformal parameter
\lambda
and the frame
\vec{e}

-\star d\lambda=\vec{e}_{1}\cdot d\vec{e}_{2}

so that we have the Wente structure

\displaystyle-\Delta_{0}\lambda=\star(d\vec{e}_{1}\cdot d\vec{e}_{2})

(4.2)

where
\Delta_{0}
denotes the negative flat Laplacian.
With the help of techiques of integration by compensation, one eventually concludes that
\lambda
is bounded from above and below, thus giving a control of the geometry of
\Sigma
.

Can the geometry of the Willmore-type hypersurface
\Sigma
associated with the energy (
1.5
) be studied by following a similar procedure to the one above?

The situation is different in four dimensions. First, we do not have an analogue of the uniformisation theorem due to the diversity of the conformal structures. Only partial answers are available in literature (see for instance
[
14
,
18
,
23
]
).
This makes it difficult to process the question above. However, if we assume that
\Sigma
is locally conformally flat then the induced metric satisfies

g=e^{4\lambda}\delta
. A similar procedure to the one above shows that the conformal parameter
\lambda
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}^{2}\lambda=e^{4\lambda}Q

(4.3)

where
Q:=\frac{1}{6}\Delta R+\frac{1}{16}\star\mbox{Pf}
is Tom Branson’s celebrated
Q-curvature

[
15
]
6
6

6

The actual expression for
Q
is
\frac{1}{6}\Delta R+\frac{1}{16}\star\mbox{Pf}-\frac{1}{4}|W|^{2}
where
W
is the Weyl curvature,
R
is the scalar curvature and Pf is the Pfaffian 4-form. It is known that
W
vanishes for a conformally flat manifold.
.
Clearly, the left hand side of (
4.3
) is a divergence. Therefore, the quantity
\frac{1}{16}\star\mbox{Pf}
must also be a divergence.
It is expected that a link should exist between a good Coulomb frame and the energy (
1.5
) .

4.3
Analysis of branched Willmore surfaces

The local analysis of point singularities of the two dimensional Willmore surfaces is motivated by the notion of concentration compactness: sequences of Willmore immersions with uniformly bounded energy converges everywhere except at finitely many points where the Willmore energy is concentrated. Such point singularities, which naturally occur as blow-ups of the Willmore flow, were first analytically investigated in codimension one by Kuwert and Schätzle (see
[
48
,
49
]
).
In
[
48
]
, they proved the regularity of Willmore surfaces having unit density singularity; and later considered a more general case of Willmore surfaces with higher order singularity
[
49
]
. Their results were generalised to higher codimension by Bernard and Rivière in
[
5
]
via the reformulation of the Willmore equation in divergence form and techniques of integration by compensation (see also
[
61
]
).

Let
\vec{\Phi}:D_{1}(0)\backslash\{0\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
be an immersion.
A point
p
on
\Sigma
is called a branch point
7
7

7

The terms
branch point
and
singularity
are similar but quite different. The immersion
\vec{\Phi}
and its derivative
d\vec{\Phi}
are both defined at branch points but
\vec{\Phi}
ceases to be an immersion there.
if
\vec{\Phi}
degenerates at
p
or if
d\vec{\Phi}
vanishes at
p
. For simplicity, we have assumed that there is a branch point at the origin
0
and
\vec{\Phi}
is smooth away from the origin but continuous there. Using Noether’s theorem,
\vec{\Phi}
satisfies an equation of the type

\displaystyle d^{\star}\vec{V}=\vec{0}

(4.4)

for some tensor
\vec{V}
.
The equation (
4.4
) hold, in particular, away from the origin.
A constant
\vec{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, called the first residue, can be defined by

\displaystyle\vec{s}_{0}:=\int_{\partial D_{1}(0)}\vec{\nu}\cdot\vec{V}

where
\vec{\nu}
is the unit outward normal vector to
\partial D_{1}(0)
. One can show that the immersion is smooth across the singularity at the origin when the residue vanishes. This condition on the residue is known as the point removability condition. Bernard and Rivière
[
5
]
showed that branched Willmore surfaces are smooth if some point removability conditions are satisfied.

It is interesting to ask if similar point removability conditions can be obtained in order to study
the four dimensional branched Willmore-type hypersurface associated with the energy (
1.5
).
Since our regularity proof of Theorem
3.0.1
is not based on integration by compensation but on a Bourgain-Brezis type result, we expect enormous difficulty in answering the question above.

Appendix A
Brief Background Notes

A.1
Geometric background

In this section, we briefly recall definitions and fundamental facts that are well known in Geometry. A comprehensive discussion can be found in most standard texts such as
[
43
]
,
[
45
]
,
[
46
]
and
[
69
]
.

A.1.1
Manifolds

A topological space
\mathcal{M}
is
locally Euclidean
if every point in
\mathcal{M}
has a neighbourhood
U
that is homeomorphic to an open subset
\Omega
of a Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The homeomorphism
f:U\rightarrow\Omega
gives rise to the pair
(U,f)
which is called a
chart
. The chart
(U,f)
is
centered at

x\in U
if
f(x)=0
. A collection
\{(U_{\alpha},f_{\alpha})\}
of charts that form an open covering of
\mathcal{M}
is known as
atlas.

A
manifold

\mathcal{M}
of dimension
m
is a locally Euclidean topological space of dimension
m
.
For the sake of precision, we give the following definition. More details can be found in
[
43
]
.

An
m
-dimensional topological manifold
\mathcal{M}
is an Hausdorff, second countable (has a countable basis),
n
-dimensional locally Euclidean space.
A
differential manifold
of class
C^{k}

(1\leq k\leq\infty)
is a topological manifold
\mathcal{M}
such that

(1)

\bigcup_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}=\mathcal{M}
.

(2)

For all
\alpha,\beta,

f_{\alpha}\circ f_{\beta}^{-1}
is
C^{k}
.

(3)

The atlas
\{U_{\alpha},f_{\alpha}\}
is maximal.

On a differentiable manifold
\mathcal{M}
, we can define a rule that takes of each
p\in\mathcal{M}
to the tangent space
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
. This rule is called a
vector field.
Thus vector fields are maps from
\mathcal{M}
onto the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{M}
. The vector field
X
is called smooth if the associated mapping is smooth. Let
U\subset\mathcal{M}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be open subsets and let
x:U\rightarrow V
be a local coordinate chart. If
X:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T\mathcal{M}
is a vector field then the restriction
X|_{U}
can be represented via

X(p)=\Sigma_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}(p)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}

where each function
a_{i}:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is smooth.

A.1.2
Submanifolds

A differentiable map
f:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is called an immersion if for any
p\in\mathcal{M}
, the differential of
f

df:T_{p}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T_{f(p)}\mathcal{N}

is injective (
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
denotes the tangent space of
\mathcal{M}
at the point
p
).

Roughly speaking submanifolds are images of injective immersions. Just as a manifold looks locally like a Euclidean space, a submanifold can also be viewed as a subset of a manifold resembling a subspace of a Euclidean space.

A.1.3
Differential forms

We fix a coordinate basis
\{dx^{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,4}
and an orientation. In order to ease the notation, we will denote the differential elements
dx^{i_{1}}\wedge_{4}...\wedge_{4}dx^{i_{k}}
by
dx^{i_{1}...i_{k}}
. A
k
-form is defined locally as

\displaystyle A=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}A_{[i_{1}...i_{k}]}\,\,dx^{i_{1}...i_{k}}

which we abbreviate as

A=\frac{1}{k!}A_{[i_{1}...i_{k}]}dx^{i_{1}...i_{k}}

where

A_{[i_{1}...i_{k}]}=\frac{1}{k!}\delta^{j_{1}...j_{k}}_{i_{1}...i_{k}}A_{j_{1}...j_{k}}.

The wedge product is defined as follows. For
A\in\Lambda^{p}
and
B\in\Lambda^{q}
, we have

A\wedge B=\frac{1}{(p+q)!}A_{[a_{1}...a_{p}}B_{b_{1}...b_{q}]}dx^{a_{1}...a_{p}b_{1}...b_{p}}\in\Lambda^{p+q}.

Defining the Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{abcd}:=|g|^{1/2}\mbox{sign}\begin{pmatrix}1&2&3&4\\
a&b&c&d\end{pmatrix}
enables us to define the Hodge star operator. For
A\in\Lambda^{p}
,

\star A=\frac{1}{(4-p)!}\epsilon_{i_{1}...i_{4}}\left(\frac{1}{p!}A^{[i_{1}...i_{p}]}\right)dx^{i_{p+1}...i_{4}}.

With this definition of
\star
, we find

\star\star A=(-1)^{p(4-p)}A.

Also, for
A\in\Lambda^{p}
and
B\in\Lambda^{q}
, we find that

\displaystyle(\star A)\wedge B

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4-p+q)!}(\star A)_{[i_{p+1}...i_{4}}\wedge B_{j_{1}...j_{q}]}dx^{i_{p+1}...i_{4}j_{1}...j_{q}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4-p+q)!}\epsilon_{i_{1}...i_{4}}\left(\frac{1}{p!}A^{[i_{1}...i_{p}]}\right)\wedge B_{j_{1}...j_{q}}dx^{i_{p+1}...i_{4}j_{1}...j_{q}}.

If
A
is a
p
-form, then

dA=\frac{1}{(p+1)!}\nabla_{[i}A_{i_{1}...i_{p}]}dx^{i}\wedge_{4}dx^{i_{1}...i_{p}},

For
A\in\Lambda^{p}
and
B\in\Lambda^{q}
, Leibnitz rule reads

d(A\wedge B)=dA\wedge B+(-1)^{p}A\wedge dB.

We also define
d^{\star}:=\star d\star.
With this definition, we find that

d^{\star}A=\frac{1}{(p-1)!}\nabla^{i_{1}}A_{[i_{1}...i_{p}]}dx^{i_{2}...i_{p}}.

In particular, for a
p
-form
A
,

\Delta A=d^{\star}dA+dd^{\star}A=\frac{1}{p!}\Delta_{g}A_{[i_{1}...i_{p}]}dx^{i_{1}...i_{p}}.

Example A.1.1
.

Let
A\in\Lambda^{2}
and
B\in\Lambda^{1}
, then

\displaystyle(\star A)\wedge B=\frac{1}{3!}(\star A)_{[kl}\wedge B_{m]}dx^{klm}=\frac{1}{6}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{klm}\frac{1}{3!}\epsilon_{ij\alpha\beta}\frac{1}{2!}A^{ij}\wedge B_{\gamma}dx^{klm}.

If we apply the Hodge star operator to the above, we have

\displaystyle\star((\star A)\wedge B)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1!}\left[\epsilon^{klmr}\frac{1}{3!}\left(\frac{1}{6}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{klm}\epsilon_{ij\alpha\beta}\left(\frac{1}{2!}A^{ij}\right)\wedge B_{\gamma}\right)\right]g_{rs}dx^{s}.

Appendix B
Some Computations I

Let
\vec{A}
and
\vec{B}
be 2-vectors. From the definition of the first-order contraction operator (see page
2.1
for definition), we have the following multiplication rule.

\displaystyle\vec{A}\cdot\vec{B}

\displaystyle:=(\vec{A}_{1}\wedge\vec{A}_{2})\cdot(\vec{B}_{1}\wedge\vec{B}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\vec{A}_{1}\cdot\vec{B}_{1})(\vec{A}_{2}\cdot\vec{B}_{2})-(\vec{A}_{1}\cdot\vec{B}_{2})(\vec{A}_{2}\cdot\vec{B}_{1})

\displaystyle\vec{A}\bullet\vec{B}

\displaystyle:=(\vec{A}_{1}\wedge\vec{A}_{2})\bullet(\vec{B}_{1}\wedge\vec{B}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\vec{A}_{2}\cdot\vec{B}_{2})\vec{A}_{1}\wedge\vec{B}_{1}-(\vec{A}_{2}\cdot\vec{B}_{1})\vec{A}_{1}\wedge\vec{B}_{2}-(\vec{A}_{1}\cdot\vec{B}_{2})\vec{A}_{2}\wedge\vec{B}_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+(\vec{A}_{1}\cdot\vec{B}_{1})\vec{A}_{2}\wedge\vec{B}_{4}.

With the rules above, we are ready for the following computation. We know that
S_{0}
and
\vec{R}_{0}
satisfy

dS_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\cdot}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad d\vec{R}_{0}=\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}+\star d\vec{u}-\vec{J}_{0}\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}.

We compute

\displaystyle\quad\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}\star\left(d\vec{R}_{0}-\star d\vec{u}+\vec{J}_{0}\wedge\star d\vec{\Phi}\right)

\displaystyle=\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}\star(\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3!}(\star\vec{\eta})_{[ab}\bullet(\star(\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}))_{r]}\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3!}\frac{1}{6}\delta_{abr}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\left(\epsilon_{\alpha\beta pq}\frac{1}{2!}\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}\right)\bullet\left(\epsilon_{\gamma ijk}\frac{1}{3!}\vec{L}^{ij}\wedge\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}\right)\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{216}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta pq}\epsilon_{\gamma ijk}\left(-g^{qk}\vec{L}^{ij}\wedge\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}-(\vec{L}^{ij}\cdot\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi})\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}\right)\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle:=\vec{A}-\frac{1}{126}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta pq}\epsilon_{\gamma ijk}\vec{B}^{ijpk}\wedge\nabla^{q}\vec{\Phi}\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}.

(B.1)

We have for the first term of (
B.1
)

\displaystyle\vec{A}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{216}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta pq}\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.74336pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.74336pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.52332pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.65952pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{q}}}_{{{\gamma ij}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.91634pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.91634pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.36322pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.688pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.74336pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.74336pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.52332pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.65952pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{q}}}_{{{\gamma ij}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.91634pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.91634pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.36322pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.688pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.74336pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.74336pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.52332pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.65952pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{q}}}_{{{\gamma ij}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.91634pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.91634pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.36322pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.688pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.74336pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.74336pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.52332pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.65952pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{q}}}_{{{\gamma ij}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.91634pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.91634pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.36322pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.688pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\vec{L}^{ij}\wedge_{4}\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{216}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta pq}\epsilon^{cijq}g_{c\gamma}\vec{L}_{ij}\wedge\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{216}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\delta_{\alpha\beta pq}^{cijq}g_{c\gamma}\vec{L}_{ij}\wedge\nabla^{p}\vec{\Phi}\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}\vec{L}_{[ab}\wedge\nabla_{r]}\vec{\Phi}\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=-\vec{L}\overset{\wedge}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{\Phi}.

(B.2)

). Note that we have set

is antisymmetric in indices

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{432}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\delta^{lmpq}_{\gamma ijk}g_{l\alpha}g_{m\beta}\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}}\wedge\nabla_{q}\vec{\Phi}\,\,\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{432}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\left(\delta^{p}_{\gamma}\delta^{lmq}_{ijk}-\delta^{q}_{\alpha}\delta^{lmp}_{ijk}\right)g_{l\alpha}g_{m\beta}\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}}\wedge\nabla_{q}\vec{\Phi}\,\,\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{72}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\left(g_{i\alpha}g_{j\beta}\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}\wedge\nabla_{k}\vec{\Phi}-g_{i\alpha}g_{j\beta}\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.6 \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{36}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}g_{i\alpha}g_{j\beta}\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}}{\vec{B}^{{{ijk}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.96301pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.69165pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.77974pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}}\wedge\nabla_{\gamma}\vec{\Phi}\,\,\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{36}\delta^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{abr}\delta^{k}_{p}\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}_{{{\alpha\beta k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}_{{{\alpha\beta k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}_{{{\alpha\beta k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}_{{{\alpha\beta k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\wedge\nabla_{\gamma}\vec{\Phi}\,\,\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}

where
\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}_{{{\alpha\beta k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}_{{{\alpha\beta k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}_{{{\alpha\beta k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{p}}}_{{{\alpha\beta k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
is antisymmetric in the indices
\alpha,\beta
and
k
.

Thus we arrive at

d\vec{R}_{0}=\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}\star d\vec{R}_{0}-\star d\vec{u}-\star\vec{\eta}\overset{\bullet}{\wedge}_{4}d\vec{u}+\star\vec{\eta}\wedge_{4}\star dS_{0}\,\,+\vec{Q}

where

\vec{Q}:=\frac{1}{12}\delta_{abr}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}(\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.61893pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.61893pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.83974pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.17123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{k\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.61893pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.61893pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.83974pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.17123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{k\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.61893pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.61893pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.83974pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.17123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{k\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.61893pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.61893pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.83974pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.17123pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{k\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}}-\mathchoice{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{k\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{k\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{k\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\vec{B}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.15334pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.6113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.15092pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{k}}}_{{{k\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.42017pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.93228pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}})\wedge\nabla_{\gamma}\vec{\Phi}\,\,dx^{a}\wedge_{4}dx^{b}\wedge_{4}dx^{r}.

Appendix C
Some Computations II

Lemma C.0.1
.

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an immersion. Let
g
and
h
be the first and second fundamental form respectively of
\Sigma
. The trace free second fundamental form satisfies the identity

(h_{0})_{ik}(h_{0})_{j}^{k}=\frac{1}{2}|h_{0}|^{2}g_{ij}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle(h_{0})_{ik}(h_{0})_{j}^{k}

\displaystyle=(h_{0})_{ik}(h_{0})^{sk}g_{sj}

\displaystyle=(h_{0})_{i1}(h_{0})^{11}g_{1j}+(h_{0})_{i1}(h_{0})^{21}g_{2j}+(h_{0})_{i2}(h_{0})^{12}g_{1j}+(h_{0})_{i2}(h_{0})^{22}g_{2j}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

where

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=g_{11}\left((h_{0})_{11}^{2}+(h_{0})_{12}^{2}\right)+g_{12}(h_{0})_{12}\left((h_{0})_{11}+(h_{0})_{22}\right)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=g_{12}\left((h_{0})_{11}^{2}+(h_{0})_{12}^{2}\right)+g_{22}(h_{0})_{12}\left((h_{0})_{11}+(h_{0})_{22}\right)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=g_{11}(h_{0})_{12}\left((h_{0})_{11}+(h_{0})_{22}\right)+g_{21}\left((h_{0})_{12}^{2}+(h_{0})_{22}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=g_{12}(h_{0})_{12}\left((h_{0})_{11}+(h_{0})_{22}\right)+g_{22}\left((h_{0})_{12}^{2}+(h_{0})_{22}^{2}\right).

Since
h_{0}
is trace free, we have
(h_{0})_{11}=-(h_{0})_{22}
. This gives

\displaystyle(h_{0})_{ik}(h_{0})_{j}^{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}\left((h_{0})_{11}^{2}+(h_{0})_{12}^{2}\right)&g_{12}\left((h_{0})_{11}^{2}+(h_{0})_{12}^{2}\right)\\
g_{21}\left((h_{0})_{12}^{2}+(h_{0})_{11}^{2}\right)&g_{22}\left((h_{0})_{12}^{2}+(h_{0})_{11}^{2}\right)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left((h_{0})_{11}^{2}+(h_{0})_{12}^{2}\right)g_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left((h_{0})_{11}^{2}+2(h_{0})_{12}^{2}+(h_{0})_{22}^{2}\right)g_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|h_{0}|^{2}g_{ij}

(C.1)

as announced.

∎

Let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an immersion. Let
\vec{Y}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{4,1}
be the conformal Gauss map of
\vec{\Phi}
into the deSitter space, defined via

\displaystyle\vec{Y}:=\vec{X}H+\vec{N}

\displaystyle\mbox{where}\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\vec{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\vec{\Phi}\\
\frac{1}{2}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}-1)\\
\frac{1}{2}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}+1)\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\,\,\,\,\vec{N}=\begin{pmatrix}\vec{n}\\
\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\Phi}\\
\vec{n}\cdot\vec{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}.

We have

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\vec{Y}=\vec{X}\nabla_{i}H+H\nabla_{i}\vec{X}+\nabla_{i}\vec{N}.

Observe that

\displaystyle H\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi}+\nabla_{i}\vec{n}

\displaystyle=H\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}g_{ik}-h_{ik}\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}

\displaystyle=-(h_{0})\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}

(C.2)

where
h_{0}
is the trace free second fundamental form.
Similarly,

\displaystyle H(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{\Phi})+(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla_{i}\vec{n})=H(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi})g_{ik}-h_{ik}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi})=-(h_{0})_{ij}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}).

Thus we have found that

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\vec{Y}=\vec{X}\nabla_{i}H+(h_{0})_{ij}\vec{Z}^{j}\quad\mbox{where}\,\,\,\vec{Z}^{j}=-\begin{pmatrix}\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}\\
(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi})\\
(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi})\end{pmatrix}=-\nabla^{j}\vec{X}.

We see that

\displaystyle\langle\vec{X},\vec{X}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{4,1}}

\displaystyle=|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}-1)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}+1)^{2}

\displaystyle=0.

Similarly

\displaystyle-\langle\vec{X},\vec{Z}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{4,1}}

\displaystyle=\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}+\frac{1}{2}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi})(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}-1)-\frac{1}{2}(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi})(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}+1)

\displaystyle=0.

Also

\displaystyle\langle\vec{Z}^{k},\vec{Z}^{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{4,1}}=(\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{m}\vec{\Phi})+(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi})(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{m}\vec{\Phi})-(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{k}\vec{\Phi})(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\nabla^{m}\vec{\Phi})=g^{km}

Thus

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla_{i}\vec{Y},\nabla_{j}\vec{Y}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{4,1}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\vec{X}\nabla_{i}H+(h_{0})_{ik}\vec{Z}^{k}\,\,,\,\,\vec{X}\nabla_{j}H+(h_{0})_{jm}\vec{Z}^{m}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{4,1}}

\displaystyle=(h_{0})_{ik}(h_{0})_{jm}g^{km}=(h_{0})_{ik}(h_{0})_{j}^{k}\overset{\eqref{des23}}{=}\frac{1}{2}|h_{0}|^{2}g_{ij}.

Thus we have

\left\langle\nabla_{i}\vec{Y},\nabla^{i}\vec{Y}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{4,1}}=|h_{0}|^{2}.

We have just shown that the Willmore energy correspond to the Dirichlet energy of
Y
in the Minkowski space, that is
\lVert d\vec{Y}\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{4,1}}^{2}=\int_{\Sigma}|h_{0}|^{2}
.
How about four dimensions? Does the energy (
1.5
) correspond to some energy involving
\vec{Y}
? A reasonable guess is to consider the biharmonic energy of
\vec{Y}
.

If we write
\Delta\vec{Y}:=\nabla^{i}\nabla_{i}\vec{Y}
, then

\displaystyle\Delta\vec{Y}=\nabla^{i}\vec{X}\nabla_{i}H+\vec{X}\Delta H+\nabla^{i}(h_{0})_{ij}\vec{Z}^{j}+(h_{0})_{ij}\nabla^{i}\vec{Z}^{j}.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\nabla^{i}\vec{X},\nabla^{k}\vec{X}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=\langle-\vec{Z}^{i},-\vec{Z}^{k}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=g^{ik}\quad,\hskip 28.45274pt\langle\nabla^{i}\vec{X},\vec{X}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=-\langle\vec{Z}^{i},\vec{X}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=0

\displaystyle\langle\nabla^{i}\vec{X},\vec{Z}^{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=-\langle\vec{Z}^{i},\vec{Z}^{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=-g^{im}\quad,\hskip 28.45274pt\langle\nabla^{i}\vec{X},\nabla^{k}\vec{Z}^{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=-\langle\vec{Z}^{i},\nabla^{k}\vec{Z}^{m}\rangle=0

\displaystyle\langle\vec{Z}^{j},\nabla^{k}\vec{Z}^{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=\nabla^{j}\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{h}^{km}=0\quad\,,\hskip 42.67912pt\langle\nabla^{i}\vec{Z}^{j},\nabla^{k}\vec{Z}^{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}=\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{km}

and

\displaystyle\langle\vec{X},\nabla^{k}\vec{Z}^{m}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}

\displaystyle=-\left[(\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{h}^{km})+\frac{1}{2}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}-1)(g^{km}+\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{h}^{km})-\frac{1}{2}(|\vec{\Phi}|^{2}+1)(g^{km}+\vec{\Phi}\cdot\vec{h}^{km})\right]=g^{km}

Now,

\displaystyle||\Delta\vec{Y}||^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\Delta\vec{Y},\Delta\vec{Y}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla^{i}\vec{X}\nabla_{i}H+\vec{X}\Delta H+\nabla^{i}((h_{0})_{ij}\vec{Z}^{j})\,\,,\,\,\,\nabla^{i}\vec{X}\nabla_{i}H+\vec{X}\Delta H+\nabla^{i}((h_{0})_{ij}\vec{Z}^{j})\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}H\nabla_{k}g^{ik}+\nabla_{i}H\nabla^{k}(h_{0})_{km}g^{im}+\Delta H(h_{0})_{km}g^{km}+\nabla^{i}(h_{0})_{ij}\nabla_{k}Hg^{kj}

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla^{i}(h_{0})_{ij}\nabla^{k}(h_{0})_{km}g^{jm}+(h_{0})_{ij}\Delta Hg^{ij}+(h_{0})_{ij}(h_{0})_{km}(\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{km})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}H\nabla^{i}H+\nabla_{i}H\nabla^{k}(h_{0})^{i}_{k}+\Delta H(h_{0})_{i}^{i}+\nabla^{i}(h_{0})_{ij}\nabla^{j}H

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla^{i}(h_{0})_{ij}\nabla^{k}(h_{0})^{j}_{k}+(h_{0})^{i}_{i}\Delta H+(h_{0})_{ij}(h_{0})_{km}(\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{km})

Noting that
(h_{0})_{i}^{i}=0
and using Codazzi equation, we find that

\displaystyle\nabla^{i}(h_{0})_{ij}\nabla^{j}H=3\nabla_{j}H\nabla^{j}H\quad\mbox{and}\quad\nabla^{i}(h_{0})_{ij}\nabla^{k}(h_{0})_{k}^{j}=9\nabla_{j}H\nabla^{j}H.

Hence,

\displaystyle||\Delta\vec{Y}||_{\mathbb{R}^{6,1}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\nabla_{i}H\nabla^{i}H+(h_{0})_{ij}(h_{0})_{km}(\vec{h}^{ij}\cdot\vec{h}^{km})

\displaystyle=-2|\nabla H|^{2}+(h_{0})_{ij}(h_{0})_{km}h^{ij}h^{km}

\displaystyle=-2|\nabla H|^{2}+(h_{0})_{ij}(h_{0})_{km}\left((h_{0})^{ij}+Hg^{ij}\right)\left((h_{0})^{km}+Hg^{km}\right)

\displaystyle=-2|\nabla H|^{2}+|h_{0}|^{4}.

Clearly the biharmonic energy of
\vec{Y}
is not the energy (
1.5
). However, it correctly reproduces the leading order term of (
1.5
).
